#3094. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#3093. OpenAI and Anthropic Drop New AI Tools for Cybersecurity, Images and Finance

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Here are three AI product drops you might've missed this week.

Every week is a busy week in the artificial intelligence industry, and this week is no exception. As voters expressed serious concerns about AI and data centers at the ballot box, OpenAI and Anthropic — makers of ChatGPT and Claude, respectively — each dropped new updates to make their chatbots a little more capable.

All these changes come amid rapidly changing government and financial environments. The midterm elections, in which Democrats handily beat Republican candidates across the country, showed that anti-AI and anti-Big Tech campaigns resonated with voters. Tech critics told CNET this week that there is a clear desire for government officials at every level to change how they interact with technology companies — to keep their campaign promises. The election results are also shaking up Wall Street, as investors’ fears about AI’s long-term financial viability grow.

Smaller updates like these are helpful for everyday AI users, and there’s a benefit for AI companies, too. They can prove they’re competitive in a market that is losing confidence in AI. Here are the three biggest AI updates to ChatGPT and Claude.

Gone are the days when every new AI tool got an immediate general release — and that’s probably for the best. Now, when a major AI lab has a new frontier AI model, it slow-rolls its access, and it starts with cybersecurity defenders. That’s exactly what OpenAI did with its newest model variant, GPT-6 Cyber.

Introducing GPT‑6 Cyber: a major advance in security research that pairs stronger capabilities with better model alignment.The model powered the Linux kernel research we announced yesterday, and security teams at companies like Mozilla, Palo Alto Networks, Calif, and… pic.twitter.com/34uAeNnhwP

GPT-6 Cyber is a version of OpenAI’s newest flagship model that’s specifically made for security research and work. OpenAI says it’s best at finding vulnerabilities and staying in its secure container. Those two things matter a lot. Earlier this fall, an OpenAI model was partially responsible for helping researchers identify a major vulnerability in PHP, a programming language that’s used in the majority of websites. And any improvements to keep AI secure are necessary. We saw many instances this summer of AI agents breaking out of their testing environments and making cybersecurity experts fear future autonomous hacks.

You won’t be able to use GPT-6 Cyber unless you’re a cybersecurity expert who’s part of OpenAI’s Daybreak program. Cisco and Mozilla teams are already using the model, according to OpenAI.

OpenAI also added a minor model update to its AI image model, called ChatGPT Images 2.6. The biggest improvement is its speed; OpenAI says the new model is 50% faster than before. That matters for AI enthusiasts who are generating multiple variations of a prompt. The new model also lets you animate your AI images into short GIFs. It’s available globally now for all ChatGPT users.

This isn’t the return of the long-dead Sora AI video model, but it is a sign that OpenAI isn’t giving up on the creative AI space entirely. Anthropic, by comparison, has never had a traditional AI image or video generator and opted for a design tool that’s better suited for mocking up websites and apps.

Anthropic is teaming up with fintech platform Plaid to help you bring your financial information into its Claude chatbot.

Both companies say they have strong security measures in place to keep your information safe. But CNET always recommends a cautious approach when it comes to sharing your financial information (or any sensitive information) with an AI product.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

#3092. 6 default Pixel 11 settings I changed that made a big difference – and why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

If you’re a new Pixel 11 user, here are six things I recommend changing right away to get the most from your phone.

I’ve been a dedicated Pixel user since the beginning, so I was naturally excited to get my hands on Google’s latest iteration, the Pixel 11 Pro XL. I’ve been impressed with it overall, but I noticed that, as Google has done before, the device isn’t set up perfectly out of the box.

Also: Pixel 11 is $200 off in this early Black Friday deal

After spending a few weeks with the device, I found that several features are either turned off by default or need optimizing to work in the best way possible.

If you’re a new Pixel 11 user, here are six things I recommend changing right away to get the most from your phone.

The camera was the biggest reason I first joined team Pixel back in 2016, so it’s one of the first places I looked on the Pixel 11 Pro XL. New this year is Camera Looks, a setting that changes how images will be processed before you take them. You can change a number of settings, including contrast, shadows, tint, sharpness, and warmth.

Also: A week with the Pixel 11 changed my mind about Gemini

I’ve always loved the camera on any Pixel I’ve had, but I’ll admit that images can look a little over-processed at times. If picture quality is important to you at all, it’s vital to set this feature right out of the box because it applies to every image you take from that point on. You can find Camera Look by opening the camera app, tapping the settings icon in the bottom-right corner, and making your changes.

Pixel’s “At a Glance” lock screen widgets are one of the most sneakily useful features, and you’re missing out if you’re not using them. These widgets are off by default, but when you enable them, you can see real-time information without unlocking your phone. You can see weather info, sports updates, flight information, rideshare and food delivery updates, fitness information, and more.

Also: These tech gifts topped our experts’ 2026 holiday wish lists

To use these widgets, long-press on an open space on your home screen. To customize your widgets, go to Settings > Wallpaper & style > More lock screen settings > Widgets on lock screen. To access your widgets, you just swipe left from your lock screen.

Out of the box, your Pixel 11’s display isn’t set to utilize its full capability. The Pixel 11 series comes with a fantastic low-temperature polycrystalline oxide (LTPO) display, but it’s slightly throttled in the name of battery life. You’ll notice the increased resolution most on text and in fine details. It won’t make a huge difference to your streaming or large display elements, though. If you read consistently, either articles or e-books, you’ll want to make the change.

To change this, head to Settings > Display > Screen Resolution. Bump the setting from “High resolution,” which is 1080 x 2404, to “Max resolution,” which is 1344 x 2992.

If you’re anything like me, your phone usually needs a quick charge some evenings. The Pixel 11 series introduced Extreme Charging Mode, which accelerates charging by pausing background activity and non-essential apps. Google says the mode should charge the Pixel 11 and 11 Pro to over 50% in 30 minutes, and the Pixel 11 Pro XL to 75% in 30 minutes. From my testing, that estimate was pretty accurate.

Also: After testing the Pixel 11, I feel fine about skipping the Pro models

There are a few caveats: this feature is only available when your phone is at or below 50% battery, and you do need to turn it on every time you use it. But after the first time you do it, it’s available in your quick settings. Head to Settings > Battery & charging > Extreme Charging to turn it on the first time.

If you have the newest Pixel, you might as well take advantage of one of its biggest selling points. I felt like this feature was gimmicky at first (and I’m still a little grumpy it replaced my beloved thermometer), but the more I use it, the more I like it. The multicolor LED on the phone’s rear camera bump can indicate who’s calling, serve as a status indicator for Gemini, and more — and it’s almost certain that more usability will arrive over time.

Where I found the most use was when I had my phone on silent. After assigning specific colors to contacts (you can do this from the contact information screen), I could easily see whether a call was worth picking up without reaching for my phone.

To make sure you’re getting the most out of your new device, head in and change your contact HiLight colors right away. It only takes a few seconds and makes deciding whether to answer a call a lot easier.

We’ve all accidentally swiped away an important notification or missed a notification in a sea of updates. The Pixel 11 offers two notification features to help you stay on top of things.

Notification History is a feature that logs every alert from the last 24 hours, including those you swipe away. This is off by default, meaning when you dismiss a notification, it’s gone forever. With this on, you get a searchable, chronological backup.

Also: Google’s big flagship is more Pro than ever

AI Notification Summaries, as the name implies, takes long multi-notification threads and stacked notifications and turns them into bullet points. This feature is powered by on-device Gemini Intelligence, and you can choose which apps to summarize.

You can find both of these under Settings > Notifications > Notification history & Notification Summaries.

#3090. This Semiconductor ETF Just Had Its Worst Day Since March 2020

The drop hastened the iShares Semiconductor ETF's slide into a bear market.

Technology stocks were drubbed on Thursday, Nov. 5, as investors priced in the impact of Tuesday's midterm election results.

Amid a blue tsunami that saw Democrats take control of both chambers of Congress and a host of gubernatorial victories, fears are rising that data center construction could stall and artificial intelligence (AI) regulations could enter the spotlight. Semiconductor stocks are epicenters of the calamity.

The iShares Semiconductor ETF just posted its worst intraday slide in over six years. Image source: Getty Images.

Just look at the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX +3.51%). The second-largest exchange-traded fund (ETF) in the semiconductor category slid 12.1% on Thursday, notching its worst intraday decline since March 2020, when stocks slumped into a bear market due to the coronavirus pandemic. Let's see if this ETF's weakness is a warning sign or a buy-on-the-dip opportunity.

Unfortunately, the iShares ETF's Nov. 5 slide isn't a one-off event. It comes on the heels of an 8.2% drop on Oct. 30. That is to say, in the span of about a week, this ETF has had its fifth- and seventh-worst intraday showings ever, and that's saying something, because the fund is more than 25 years old. The fund is now down 30% over the past month and trades nearly 38% below its 52-week high. In short, it's in a bear market.

Yes, election results make for an easy scapegoat for chip stocks' recent misfortunes, but there are also some stock-specific issues hindering this ETF. For example, Qualcomm (QCOM +5.53%), the fund's eighth-largest holding, tumbled on Thursday after reporting a 26% decline in fiscal fourth-quarter smartphone chip sales.

Micron Technology (MU +5.12%) is another offender hailing from this ETF's roster. That stock, the iShares fund's fourth-largest component, plunged 17% on Thursday amid fears that memory chip shortages could pinch the broader tech sector and that, if data centers stall, demand for memory chips will slump. Memory chip demand is pertinent to the iShares ETF because the fund holds several other memory names in addition to Micron.

Add in election results that some experts say represent a "total repudiation" of AI, and these are perilous times for semiconductor stocks and related ETFs.

There's no denying that "bloodbath" is an accurate description of the week for semiconductor stocks, but in turbulent times, perspective is most valuable. On that note, investors interested in kicking in the tires on the iShares ETF or individual chip names may be heartened to know that it's not uncommon for markets to immediately overreact to election results before cooler heads prevail over the subsequent weeks and months.

Second, and admittedly this is a wild card, anti-AI/data center stances make for good campaign trail fodder, but post-election reality is different for politicians. That reality includes the burning desire (in most cases) to win the next election, and railing against cash-rich tech companies that can fund opposition in primary or general elections may not be the ticket to future political victory for this year's winners.

So there may be an opportunity with chip stocks and ETFs, and the silver lining is that prospective buyers acting in the near term will benefit from better pricing than was available just a few weeks ago.

#3089. 92% of HR say staff can train for AI jobs – but half of companies still hire outside

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Most HR professionals say they believe employees can be reskilled for AI jobs, yet companies still often seek outside hires. Here's why.

HR leaders are confident that current employees can be trained for jobs created by artificial intelligence. Organizations are hiring externally instead.

That’s the finding in new research from Careerminds, a global provider of workforce solutions. The study, published in Careerminds’ white paper, The AI Redeployment Gap: What Companies Lose by Hiring over Reskilling, reveals that 92% of HR professionals and 88% of people managers believe employees within their organizations could be reskilled for AI-related roles.

Also: Nearly 70% of workers use AI regularly now – but many get no time to upskill

Yet 51% of HR professionals and 47% of people managers report that their organizations are hiring externally for new AI-related positions, compared with just 42% in each group who report redeploying existing employees.

The research was conducted in partnership with Censuswide and surveyed 595 US HR professionals and 547 US people managers, ages 25–65, working at organizations with 500-10,000 or more employees. The research was conducted May 15–26, 2026.

Also: Businesses finally seeing AI ROI, but 62% can’t handle the storage demands

“Companies are investing in AI while potentially overlooking one of their most valuable resources: the people they already employ,” said Mark Saddic, senior partner at Careerminds, in a press release.

“Our research shows that organizations largely believe their employees can adapt to AI-driven change, but that confidence isn’t consistently translating into opportunities. Before looking outside the organization, leaders need to understand the skills they already have, identify where employees can grow, and establish a clear process for making those transitions possible.”

Reskilling, especially for AI, isn’t easy to track. Limited visibility into individual skill sets and which employees should be reskilled was cited as a potential barrier by 43% of HR professionals and 34% of managers.

Also: Pearson’s Workera deal targets a big workplace problem: No time to AI upskill

It’s not the first time that the management of skills development has come up regarding AI. In September, educational giant Pearson agreed to acquire Workera, the AI-native skills platform used by organizations such as ServiceNow, Accenture, and the US Space Force.

Careermind’s data revealed that 87% of HR pros and 84% of managers believe their organizations are adequately considering reskilling employees for future roles. However, the findings suggest that these organizations may believe they are prioritizing internal talent development, even though their hiring practices reveal otherwise.

Response to AI-related workforce needs isn’t equal across the board. The findings reveal significant differences in how industries are responding to these needs.

Also: Your AI-polished resumes are recruiters’ new top stressor

For example, in the technology and telecom industries, external hiring outpaces redeployment by 15%. At the same time, media and entertainment is the only sector surveyed in which both groups report redeployment at least as frequently as external hiring. Among HR professionals in that industry, 56% report redeploying employees, compared with just 25% who hire externally.

Separate research from The Josh Bersin Company, cited in the Careerminds white paper, reveals that external hiring can cost three to five times more than filling comparable roles internally — and on average takes up to 20 days longer.

#3088. CNET People’s Picks: Readers Say This Brand Wins as the Top Laptop Choice

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

If you prefer a PC, there are still some non-Apple brands worth considering.

Shopping for a new laptop for the holidays, but not sure which brand to choose from? Our readers do.

We recently surveyed CNET readers as part of our People’s Picks awards to find out what they think about their laptop brand to help you make a more sound choice for your next laptop. We listed several well-known names, including Dell and Lenovo. We asked about everything that matters most on a daily basis, like battery life, durability and even its performance for handling tasks like gaming and graphics. Out of 10 categories, one brand wins most of them: Apple.

Apple swept up eight of the awards for its signature MacBooks and claimed the overall winner for CNET’s People’s Picks award. Here’s why.

Apple wins the overall award in CNET’s People’s Picks, and also takes home the overall satisfaction rating, with 74.2% of Apple users saying they’re extremely satisfied with their laptop. In comparison, 22.5% say they’re satisfied — bringing Apple to a near-perfect overall satisfaction score of 96.7%.

Apple’s overall impression also speaks to other awards it wins from CNET readers, including high ratings for its impressive battery life, display quality and high-quality build. All of those categories and more sum up Apple’s brand loyalty nod, with 68% of MacBook owners saying they would buy the same laptop and model from Apple again.

How much are people paying for MacBooks? CNET found that 43.5% of Apple users paid at least $1,500. Until earlier in 2026, the MacBook Air was Apple’s entry-level laptop, priced at least $1,299. The introduction of the budget-friendly MacBook Neo brought that down to a more reasonable $699. However, despite Apple’s laptops being more expensive than those from other brands, they still ranked high for their value proposition. Part of the reason for that is that MacBooks received high marks for hardware reliability. “Apple’s MacBooks might cost more than the average laptop, but their premium build quality means they’re built for the long haul,” said Matt Elliott, CNET’s laptop editor.

If you plan to have your laptop for a while, you may be impressed by other features that make work easier and faster, like its M-series central processing unit for task management and AI capabilities, great battery life and a one-of-a-kind operating system — currently MacOS 27. All of the features and specifications bundled together make Apple’s perception as a high-end laptop worth the investment for long-term satisfaction.

Apple clinches a near-perfect score for its value perception. According to our survey results, 44% of Apple users said their MacBook has exceptional value. Others say their MacBook is worth what they paid for it (50.9%) — for a total positive score of 94.6%.

Digging deeper into Apple’s cost-to-value rating, 81.8% of Apple owners paid at least $1,000 for their laptops, which may seem costly, but Apple users seem OK with the price. That could be because, even though Apple has only three laptop models in a handful of sizes in its lineup, each one delivers solid performance and storage.

Take Apple’s proprietary processing chips, for example — each upgrade since the M1 release has improved the laptop’s speed, battery life and performance. Now the M6 can handle the MacBook’s AI capabilities, storage and high-resolution graphics. And for all of that power, most MacBooks are relatively thin and lightweight, making them easier for mobility — just a few of the reasons Apple users may be OK with paying more for what they’re getting.

Battery life is a big deal for many laptop buyers, and that’s another area where Apple delivers. Over a quarter of those surveyed (25.7%) said their MacBook’s battery life exceeds 10 hours — which is more than enough for a full day of work in most cases. And 18.7% say their MacBook’s battery lasts between 8 and 10 hours — totaling 44.4% of MacBook users saying their battery lasts 8 hours or more.

Comparatively, non-Apple laptop brands fall short in this category. Only 16.4% of Lenovo users say their laptop’s battery lasts at least 8 hours, and even fewer for Dell (12.7%) and HP (11.8%). If you’re leaning toward a MacBook for its battery life, CNET’s lab test shows that the MacBook Neo lasts 13 hours and 26 minutes for online streaming, whereas the Apple MacBook Air 13-inch M5 (2026 model) lasts 17 hours and 2 minutes.

Apple sweeps the brightness, color accuracy and clarity category with 99.2% of its users either satisfied or extremely satisfied with their laptop’s screen performance. No Apple users reported dissatisfaction.

“Apple has long been a favorite for creative professionals and content creators for good reason: Its MacBook displays are some of the best you’ll find on any laptop,” said Josh Goldman, CNET’s managing editor for computers. “While Apple may use marketing names like Retina and Liquid Retina to describe display features, they do translate into sharp and clear text and images, with excellent brightness, contrast, color gamut coverage and color accuracy.”

The new MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR even goes beyond what MacBook users have come to expect. It’s Apple’s first OLED touchscreen laptop and features next-level brightness, color and contrast.

Apple receives high ratings: 80% of MacBook owners say it has premium build quality, 18.2% say it’s solid, and those results don’t surprise us.

And MacBooks seem durable and reliable, with 87% of Apple users reporting no hardware failures on their MacBooks, while 8% say minor software fixes resolved their issues. Only 3% needed minor hardware repairs, and 2.2% needed major repairs — which could be fixes such as a battery or screen replacement.

Our editors say the M5 MacBook Air is thin and lightweight, but still has a well-built aluminum design. And the M5 Pro MacBook Pro is sturdy, too. What sets Apple’s laptop hardware apart could be its aluminum chassis — the casing that holds all of the components together.

“Apple makes three lines of MacBooks — the Neo, Air and Pro — and focuses on making those the best they can possibly be. They are all built to deliver a premium experience using high-quality materials, and that even includes the entry-level MacBook Neo,” said Goldman.

Lastly, Apple uses durable materials, such as the 100% recycled aluminum alloy it says encloses the 14-inch MacBook Pro. And the tech giant has several testing facilities to test MacBook durability.

When it comes to getting your daily tasks done, most CNET readers with MacBooks (70.9%) say Apple’s laptops handle workloads flawlessly. It’s worth noting that 45% use their MacBook for work tasks, but no Apple users reported poor performance.

Here’s why that might be: Apple’s laptops are packed with top-notch performance specs and features, like their powerful processors, unified memory and fast storage. Better processor performance means enhanced graphics, on-device AI capabilities and faster task speeds, all of which add up to being able to finish work, play games or stream videos without issues or lags caused by your laptop’s hardware.

On the software side, MacOS 27 Golden Gate gives you built-in AI features, like Siri AI and Visual Intelligence. And you can organize your Safari tabs by topic and use the Notify Me tool to be notified of website changes, like price drops or breaking news. Plus, you can create shortcuts to automate tasks. Coupling all of these features and specifications explains why Apple users rate MacBooks so highly for getting the job done.

Apple leads the way on brand retention with 68% of Apple users planning to buy the same MacBook model again, while 29.1% would buy another model from Apple’s lineup. Only 3% would switch to another brand that’s not Apple.

Usually, when you buy one Apple product, you’re likely to stick to the brand’s ecosystem, especially since Apple has its own operating systems to access its unique features and work seamlessly with other Apple products with Apple’s Continuity feature. For example, you can start an email on your iPhone, but finish typing it on your MacBook. Or you can call or send a text from your laptop without reaching for your iPhone. Other features work across Apple devices, including iCloud, Universal Clipboard and AirDrop.

Asus wins as a midtier laptop choice with 67% of Asus owners paying between $500 and $1,499. There’s a sweet spot in this range with 38% spending between $500 and $999, and 28% paying between $1,000 and $1,499.

If you’re planning to spend that amount on a new laptop, you may also be pleased to know that Asus earns a higher satisfaction rating compared with other non-Apple laptop brands — 48% of Asus users say they’re extremely satisfied.

Asus laptops have a few features and specifications that may speak to that rating, regardless of budget. For example, the Zenbook A14 has a thin, lightweight design that’s rigid, with long battery life and an OLED display with strong contrast and color performance. This Copilot Plus PC sells for $1,600 (but retailers like Best Buy regularly have it on sale for less). Comparatively, these premium features from other brands could cost more or sacrifice important must-have features for the price.

Asus has other laptops worth exploring depending on your needs, like the budget-friendly Asus ZenBook S 14 or the premium Asus ROG Zephyrus G14 that could be good for gamers and creators.

Over one-third of Acer laptop owners (38%) paid less than $500 for their laptop — which is greater than other laptop brands in this price range. Yet, 44% of Acer users say their laptop has exceptional value, while 50% say the value is fair — bringing a total positive cost-to-value score of over 94%. Acer also has an overall satisfaction score of 92.6% — the highest of non-Apple laptop brands.

If you’re impressed by Acer’s value and satisfaction scores, there are a few budget-friendly Acer options to consider. The Acer Aspire 16 AI wins CNET’s Editors’ Choice award. It has a big 16-inch screen but is still lightweight (3.5 pounds). And it has good battery life (over 21 hours on CNET’s battery drain test). There are a few other pluses, like the 1440p web camera that may be good for video conferencing and a sizable touchpad. All of those features and more add up to $700, which could make it a good computer for work or casual browsing. If you’re looking to spend slightly less, its predecessor, the Aspire 14 AI, is originally $639, but you may be able to score deals on both models during holiday sales.

Even though Apple is highly recommended by CNET readers, there are plenty of other laptop models and brands worth considering depending on your needs. Here are a few non-Apple laptop brands that are runner-ups to CNET’s People’s Picks winners that you shouldn’t count out of your laptop search.

Lenovo is best known for packing a powerful laptop into a compact design with tablet functionality, like its Yoga line, and its durable ThinkPad business models. But CNET readers with Lenovo laptops say the brand’s battery life stands out most compared with other non-MacBook laptops.

Survey findings show that 16% of Lenovo owners say their laptops can run for 8 hours or more. And 10.5% say their laptop battery lasts between 8 and 10 hours, while 6% say their Lenovo laptops can withstand at least 10 hours before needing a charge. That’s more than what readers say about other non-Apple brands. The Yoga 9i 2-in-1 Aura Edition lasts 25 hours and 45 minutes — making it one of the best laptop batteries CNET experts have tested.

Goldman said Lenovo has such a large and varied PC selection using the latest chips from Intel, AMD, Qualcomm and Nvidia that there is something for everyone and every budget. Even those who spend less on a Lenovo laptop can expect solid performance, including battery life.

If your laptop’s hardware fails or it breaks, you may be in the market for a new laptop sooner than expected. Think about cooling fan problems, overheating or your laptop not powering on at all. Over half (68%) of Lenovo laptop owners said they haven’t had any hardware issues with their laptops — the most compared to other non-MacBooks.

Beyond hardware durability, Lenovo also scores highly for its build and construction — overall making it a sturdy laptop choice. In our survey, 38% of Lenovo laptop owners say their laptop has a premium feel, while 51% say it’s solid — totaling a positive score of 89%.

If you’re looking for recommendations, the Lenovo Yoga Slim 7x Gen 11 has an all-metal design. And the Lenovo ThinkPad X1 Carbon Gen 14 is a business laptop with Lenovo’s well-known X1 Carbon exterior and new Space Frame design, which Elliott says makes it easier to repair or replace parts if needed.

Many Lenovo laptop users are loyal to the brand, which speaks to the overall positive satisfaction rating of 83%. If Lenovo owners were in the market for a new laptop, nearly three in four (73.2%) say they would buy another Lenovo laptop. Breaking down the percentage, 33.6% say they would buy the same model again. Meanwhile, 39.6% would buy a different model but stick to Lenovo.

Here’s why that might be. Lenovo offers a plethora of options to choose from based on your needs — while still evolving its laptops. For instance, the Lenovo Legion 5i Gen 10 has an open M.2 slot for a second storage drive and an OLED screen for crisp, colorful images — making it perfect for gaming. Its Yoga two-in-ones let you seamlessly switch between a tablet and traditional laptop, while Lenovo’s ThinkPad and ThinkBook business laptops offer everything from ultralight 2-pound bodies to expanding rollable screens. And its new designs don’t sacrifice the must-have needs like long battery life, durability and chip efficiency that lend to strong performance.

Asus wins over nearly half of its laptop owners, with 47.5% giving it an exceptional value rating  — exceeding expectations for its price. Take our Editors’ Choice pick, the Asus Zenbook A14. It’s super thin and lightweight, but it’s still sturdy thanks to its Ceralumnium body. Plus, it has supreme 24-hour battery life, 1TB of storage and an OLED display — all for $1,200. While that’s more expensive than some laptops, it’s what’s included in that price that makes it worthwhile compared with other non-Apple laptop brands.

Asus is the top non-Apple laptop option to consider for tasks, with 47.7% of CNET readers giving it high ratings. That’s followed by 40.4% of Asus laptop owners who say it has very good task performance. When it comes to tasks, 37% primarily use their Asus laptops for work, while 5% use theirs for gaming.

Asus is well known for having lightweight laptops with all-day-and-night battery life and stunning display quality. Asus laptops like the Asus ROG Zephyrus G16 have a good port selection, which could be good for gaming, work or simply connecting different devices without the need for additional adapters. And the Asus Zenbook S 14 has a strong neural processing unit that contributes to its high-speed AI performance.

Asus scores highest for its screen brightness and clarity, with over half (51%) extremely satisfied with its screen quality and brightness, while 38.6% say they’re satisfied, giving Asus an overall satisfaction rate of 90%. One common thread we noticed in Asus laptops is their OLED screens, which deliver high-resolution video and graphics. Asus laptops also combine stunning display quality with excellent performance and at surprisingly low prices for all of their features.

For example, the Asus Zenbook 14 isn’t the highest-quality OLED we’ve seen, but it still produces clear text and graphics on a 14-inch screen, along with exceptional black levels, Elliott said. That’s still better than LCDs that many other non-MacBooks have, and could be the reason Asus owners rate it so highly in this category.

Dell wins our readers over as a top choice for high-end laptops. One-third (33.3%) of Dell owners spent over $1,500, and of that percentage, 15.2% spent over $2,000.

Dell’s premium laptops largely cater to business and gaming with their standout features and performance. The Alienware 16X Aurora is Dell’s midrange gaming laptop with user-replaceable RAM, a bright display and strong performance for $2,280. But if you want more gaming features, Dell has the Alienware 16 Area-51, which has even better gaming performance, upgradable RAM and three SSD slots for $2,530. And if you’re looking for a non-Mac alternative that comes close to MacBook quality and performance, the higher-end Dell XPS 14 is the one to choose. It has the Intel Core Ultra X7 358H CPU, Intel Arc B390 graphics and 1TB of storage for $1,950.

#3087. Apple will monitor your public iCloud links now, starting with iOS 27.3

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Under Apple's new policy, the company will scan publicly shared iCloud links for harmful content - and possibly ban violators.

Be careful what you share in your iCloud. Apple announced it will start monitoring public iCloud links for harmful content — and potentially disable accounts that violate company policy.

Also: How to free up your iPhone storage almost immediately

The change comes with the impending release of Apple’s 27.3 build across its operating systems. If an iCloud user selects “Anyone with the link” when sharing something in a group chat or Discord server, it’s considered a public link; Apple is within its rights to scan for anything that violates company policy or is potentially illegal.

If you try to upload harmful content, Apple says it reserves the right to “move, refuse, modify, remove, or delete” your content at any time. Furthermore, Apple outlines possible actions it may take, including:

Apple’s announcement did not explain what “harmful content” would mean in practice. While there are obvious violations like drug sales and child sex abuse material, the new policy’s vagueness is concerning.

Once an iCloud link is shared under the “Anyone with a link” designation, end-to-end encryption is removed, even if either party has enabled Advanced Data Protection, which allows Apple to view the link’s content.

If you select “People you choose,” which limits access to users who have been invited or approved, the encryption remains in place, and Apple won’t be able to see what you’re sharing.

The new policy does not lay out an appeals process if you think your account or content has been falsely flagged as harmful. However, Apple does have a web page that provides information on how to appeal for other reasons. So there is a framework in place to push back if you think you’ve been caught in the dragnet by accident.

#3086. Do You Speak Geek? 5 Tech Terms That Endure, and 5 That Got Defragged

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Level up your vocab with these tech phrases that'll give you major rizz.

Real talk: I’m not that up to speed on today’s tech vocabulary. That realization clocked me over the head this week when the Scotland-based Collins dictionary came out with its list of new and notable words for 2026. The words include p(doom), which refers to the probability of AI-caused doom; mogging, meaning to outshine or upstage someone; friendslop, a type of low-budget video game played among friends; and ghost jobs, jobs advertised with no real position or intent to hire someone.

Vibe-coding, which was Collins’ Word of the Year for 2025, refers to a conversational way to teach an AI model without typing in all that pesky code. Enshittification, which the American Dialect Society chose as its 2023 Word of the Year, means the gradual, profit-driven decline in the quality of, well, I guess, everything.

Maybe you know and use these terms regularly; maybe you don’t. But, in an effort to stop myself from becoming that Steve Buscemi faking-it-in-high-school meme, I wanted to lay out some tech terms that have a permanent home in the popular lexicon and the words, phrases and abbreviations that haven’t quite stood the test of time. (Note to self: Stop saying “emoticon.”)

Selfie: You know what a selfie is; my mother, on the other hand, may not. The word for taking a photo of yourself with any camera-enabled device was first coined in 2002 on an Australian internet chat forum, of all places, but didn’t become mainstream until 2013.

Content creator: “Influencer” also works here, to refer to a person who shares information on a tech platform and builds an audience of online followers. A related word, “parasocial,” refers to the one-sided relationship listeners or viewers may have with the creator or influencer, who, in contrast, doesn’t know they exist.

Podcast: The Oxford Dictionary named podcast the Word of the Year back in 2005. Since then, the term, which is a mashup of iPod and broadcast and refers to a digital program made available on-demand for streaming or download, has remained relevant. The iPod, however? Not so much. It was discontinued in 2022.

WFH (work from home): The trend of working from your own home via computer saw a substantial uptick during and after the 2020 COVID-19 pandemic. According to a 2022 survey by the US Bureau of Labor Statistics, 23% of Americans work remotely.

Unfriend: The New Oxford American Dictionary‘s 2009 Word of the Year became popularized thanks to Facebook, but the term that means ending a friendship has actually been around for quite some time. NPR called it “the Sleeping Beauty of words,” as the term dates back to the 17th century, when it basically meant the same thing.

Metaverse: Remember when Mark Zuckerberg poured like $80 billion into a virtual reality project that was being touted as the internet platform of the future? The metaverse and its flagship app, Horizon Worlds, were announced in 2021. Five years later, it all fell apart. Now, Zuck is investing a ton in AI, which he’s touting as the internet technology of the future. Second verse, same as the first?

NFT: Just a couple of years ago, you couldn’t go online without reading about NFTs. Nonfungible tokens are digital identifiers to certify ownership of, well, a digital thing of value. Heck, remember when President Donald Trump released an NFT? After the boom in popularity, and the subsequent crash in value of most of them, the term NFT has all but left the public consciousness.

World Wide Web: It’s the internet, folks. Or, the information superhighway if you’re nasty.

Defrag: Back in my day, when we needed better performance from our Windows-based computers, we’d defragment the hard drive. There were plenty of tools you could use to do so. Apparently, this is no longer a thing people do, which is fun, since I still like to use the term in everyday situations. While we’re at it, I’ll be right back. I need to go defrag the car.

Emoticon: Technically, emoticons are made from standard keyboard characters read sideways, such as 🙂 to generate a smiley face. Emoji are graphical images that you can select in various communication programs. (Sometimes, if you try to type an old-school emoticon, the program you’re working in will just grab it and turn it into an emoji.) These days, we just say emoji, even if the word sounds more like a dessert.

→ Baca artikel asli

#3085. Thinking Machines’ new model takes the lead in western open-weight AI

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Another competitive model developed outside of China could change the open-weight landscape.

Thinking Machines Lab, a small AI startup from ex-OpenAI CTO Mira Murati, has released its latest model, Inkling 2 Small — and according to third-party tester Artificial Analysis, it’s set to give Chinese models a run for their lead. It’s technically proprietary until now, but Thinking Machines said it will release its weights next week.

Also: Open weights vs. closed: An AI civil war’s afoot

Chinese companies have led the pack on open models over the last few years. Models from DeepSeek and Moonshot regularly top leaderboards and rival the capabilities of much more expensive, closed models from OpenAI and Anthropic.

Since DeepSeek’s R1 performed similarly to OpenAI’s o1 in January 2025, Chinese open models have given investors and high-paying enterprise customers reasons to rethink their expensive contracts with US-based labs. Western labs creating more competitive open models changes the landscape.

Thinking Machines’ latest model has 594 billion parameters and supports image inputs but outputs only text. The company noted its web and game design capabilities and that it evaluated the model’s approach to coding problems alongside the code it generated during testing.

“The result is a model that is both stronger at coding and easier to use inside real agent loops,” the company said.

Also: Nvidia takes another step forward for US open-weight AI

Inkling 2 Small scored a 31 on the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index, a composite test “for tracking progress toward artificial general intelligence” that combines benchmark scores spanning coding, science, and general categories. A score of 31 is above average for models in its class and is tied with Gemini 3.8 Flash. For context, Gemini 4 Carbon and Claude Opus 5.5 top the index at 60 and 59, respectively.

Thinking Machines said it will release the model’s weights next week, a practice that is becoming customary. Given the heightened security concerns around AI in general, AI companies spend a few weeks testing the to-be-open model with cybersecurity teams and safety reviewers before releasing the weights, rather than leading with the weights out of the gate.

French AI lab Mistral recently released its Le Chonk model via the same staggered process — it now keeps pace with OpenAI’s GPT-6.1 Terra in coding abilities, which is rare for western-made open models.

Also: Mistral’s Le Chonk is now a top open model for coding

“[Thinking Machines’] plan to release its weights would make it the highest-scoring open weights model developed outside China based on current rankings,” Artificial Analysis said.

“Inkling 2 Small is amongst the leading models in intelligence and reasonably priced when comparing to other models of similar price. It’s also notably slow per task and very verbose,” Artificial Analysis said. The organization also called Inkling 2 Small’s pricing “somewhat expensive” at $0.30 per one million input tokens, since the median cost for its class is $0.24.

Still, the model’s progress over its predecessor stands out to Artificial Analysis reviewers: Inkling 2 Small jumped 41% in automation capabilities and 20% in coding abilities. However, most models excel in certain areas or on specific benchmarks, making it hard to deem one or another the outright best coding model overall.

Also: I tried replacing Claude Code with a local AI stack that’s completely free

Inkling 2 Small also made some gains in accuracy, Artificial Analysis said.

“On AA-Omniscience v1.1, the hallucination rate falls from Inkling Small’s 61% to 27%, while accuracy rises from 32% to 34% and the attempt rate falls from 77% to 53%,” its review reported.

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tesla says it will wind down its most notorious model at Gigafactory Texas to make room for its self-driving Cybercab.

Tesla CEO Elon Musk announced the end of the brand’s most ostentatious model, the Cybertruck, in a post on X late Thursday night. Tesla will begin winding down production of the wedge-shaped pickup at its Texas Gigafactory “around the middle of next year,” Musk said, to boost manufacturing capacity for its Cybercab robotaxi.

Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production

The Cybertruck will not be missed by many. Sales have been so low that Tesla doesn’t even separate them out in its financials — lumping it into an “Other models” column — with experts estimating numbers significantly lower than Tesla’s built-out manufacturing capacity and far below Musk’s sales goals. Meanwhile, Tesla and Musk have increasingly shifted their focus to AI, robotics and autonomy as the brand’s future.

Tesla unveiled the edgy, all-electric pickup truck at an event in 2019, and the cracks were already beginning to show in many of Musk’s most outlandish claims surrounding the truck. (In the case of the “bulletproof” glass, the cracks literally showed.)

After multiple delays, deliveries finally began in 2023. Musk had spent the intervening four years baffling people by making (and subsequently retracting) many truly bizarre statements about the EV’s capabilities, but the first nail in the Cybertruck’s coffin was the starting price, which had ballooned to $60,990, up from the initial $40,000 target touted at debut.

Over its lifetime, the Cybertruck struggled to meet even the most basic expectations, contending with reliability issues, multiple recalls and a penchant for eating its owners’ fingers. It didn’t help that the sci-fi-styled EV arrived around the same time Musk became deeply involved in US politics and policy, and has become known as something of a rolling dog whistle for those who agree with the billionaire’s increasingly unpopular ideologies.

Customers were presented with a niche EV that was more expensive than expected, unsafe, unreliable, unpopular and unattractive. It’s no surprise that sales have suffered, with some experts predicting that since its arrival on US roads, Tesla has sold only about 10% of Musk’s Cybertruck goals. That’s despite Tesla building out an estimated 125,000-unit annual manufacturing capacity at the Texas Gigafactory where the truck is built, leaving the automaker with a huge stock of unsold, unwanted inventory sitting on its lots. One of Musk’s other companies, SpaceX, bought $131 million worth of Cybertrucks last year.

The Cybertruck’s 2027 wind-down will make way for increased capacity for the fully autonomous Cybercab, which debuted in 2024 and is slated to ramp up production in Texas next year. Pitched as a robotaxi that you could own for “under $30,000,” Tesla began offering rides in its steering-wheel-free Cybercab in limited parts of Austin in early September — a stunt that immediately prompted a federal investigation into whether the self-driving vehicle meets federal safety standards.

The Cybertruck joins the Model S and X on Tesla’s 2026 chopping block. The electric sedan and SUV were discontinued in January to make room for production of the Optimus humanoid robot at Tesla’s factory in Fremont, California. That will leave just the Model 3 and Model Y in Tesla’s consumer car lineup until the brand’s next rolling publicity stunt, the high-flying Tesla Roadster, allegedly arrives in 2028.

#3083. AMD or Nvidia: Which AI Chip Stock Would I Buy With $1,000 Today?

The leading GPU makers are two of the top AI investments in the market.

AMD (AMD +11.04%) and Nvidia (NVDA +2.77%) are two of the largest competitors in the AI processor industry. The third primary one is Broadcom (AVGO +0.74%), but its niche in that segment differs from theirs. Nvidia and AMD compete in the same field, designing powerful general-purpose chips while Broadcom designs highly specialized computing units for specific AI workloads.

AMD's share price has more than doubled so far this year, seriously outperforming Nvidia, which is just about flat. However, what matters more for investors is what's ahead. So, if you've got $1,000 ready to invest, is AMD or Nvidia the better stock to buy today? I think it's clearly Nvidia, and the numbers speak for themselves.

On Nov. 3, AMD reported its fiscal Q3 earnings. Nvidia's fiscal quarters end one month later than AMD's, so we'll have to wait until Nov. 17 to hear from Nvidia. However, AMD's results were pretty strong.

What investors care about most when comparing these two are their data center divisions. For AMD, this segment saw revenue growth of 97% year over year to $8.5 billion. While that was a strong result, it was still a slowdown from Q2's 107% growth to $6.7 billion.

When Nvidia reports on Nov. 17, investors will be looking closely to see if it follows suit with a growth slowdown, or if it continues to report accelerating revenue gains. During Nvidia's fiscal Q2, its data center revenue rose 117% year over year to $89 billion. So, despite sales that were more than 10 times larger than AMD's, it was growing significantly faster. If it can keep up the trend of growing faster than AMD, I think this scores a major point in Nvidia's favor, as it's far harder for such a massive business to grow that fast.

However, Nvidia's overall business is far more leveraged to the state of the data center segment than AMD is. In fiscal Q2, Nvidia's total revenue was $96.2 billion, so its $89 billion data center revenue made up over 90% of its total. In Q3, AMD's $8.5 billion total was well over half of AMD's $13.3 billion, but its other segments make a much more meaningful contribution to its results. This could be a bad thing for AMD if data center spending takes off over the next few years (like it's projected to do), or it could be a good thing if the data center build-out slows due to new laws and regulations being passed.

Regardless, Nvidia still has the growth edge here, but it also holds another huge advantage.

In 2026, AMD and Nvidia's businesses have done quite well and are delivering similar levels of growth. However, AMD's stock has soared while Nvidia's has stagnated. While I'm not sure why there is a performance split between these two, it has revealed some pricing discrepancies in the stocks.

As we get closer to turning the calendar to 2027, I think valuing the stocks based on next year's earnings estimates is a smart idea. AMD trades at 30 times next year's expected earnings, which is a bit expensive. However, Nvidia trades at just under 13 times next year's earnings, which is an absolute bargain price.

AMD PE Ratio (Forward 1y) data by YCharts.

So, AMD is far more expensive than Nvidia, and I think that's the final piece in the puzzle here. It's not often you can buy shares of a company that is by far the largest name in its industry and also growing faster than its top competitor at a huge discount to the competition, but that's exactly what's going on with Nvidia's stock.

As a result, I think that Nvidia is the best semiconductor stock to invest $1,000 in right now. AMD has had a strong run this year, but now it's about time for the leader to reassert itself. I think Nvidia's 2027 stock performance could mirror AMD's performance in 2026, and if I'm right, its shareholders will be well rewarded.

Keithen Drury has positions in Broadcom and Nvidia. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Advanced Micro Devices, Broadcom, and Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > Android

'Notify when left behind' is making its way to Android - but to limited devices for now.

A much-needed update — “Notify when left behind” — is rolling out to Google’s Find My hub. The feature sends a notification when a registered device is separated from your phone, except when you leave it at your house.

Also: 7 Galaxy settings I always change to boost my battery life

According to 9to5Google, this feature was first previewed in October, with the notification available for Chipolo trackers and Moto Tag.

This feature is not yet available for other peripherals such as headphones. I tried to see if my Sony WH-1000XM6 headphones or Google Pixel Buds Pro 2 have it — they don’t. Hopefully, support is on the way. In the meantime, here’s what we know.

“Notify when left behind” is my all-time favorite device tracking feature on iOS. Any device set up on my Find My network is compatible with this feature, including my MacBook Air, AirPods Pro 3, and Apple Watch, three devices I often take out of the house — and have indeed left behind once or twice.

Also: Google’s Pixel Tag tracker has a big advantage over AirTags

If these devices are separated from my phone in an unsaved location, I’ll get a notification of which device I left and where. In iOS, you can customize which locations are “safe,” and won’t notify you if you leave them there.

On Android, this feature is currently limited to only notifying you when a device is left at a location other than the home address registered to your Google account. Hopefully, location customization is on the horizon.

Right now, this feature appears to be limited to specific Bluetooth trackers. Photos shared by 9to5Google show compatible trackers have a “Notify when left behind” tab at the bottom of their menu in the Find Hub, so it’s expected that future supported devices will operate the same way.

9to5Google reports that previous updates to Google Play Services indicated this feature’s existence. According to 9to5Google, “Notify when left behind” should be a feature that users can enable when registering a new device with their Find Hub.

Also: I’m an Apple Maps fan, but I switch to Google Maps for these 3 tasks

Once Google matures and fully rolls out this feature, there should be a setting inside the Find Hub to enable these notifications on every device in your hub.

Android’s breadth of compatibility with third-party manufacturers is its most significant advantage over Apple. Regarding device tracking, you can add Sony headphones, Motorola trackers, and Samsung Galaxy smartwatches to the same Find Hub.

Also: Apple has a Bluetooth problem, and Android is taking advantage

However, my Beats headphones that appear in my Find My hub are not currently supported by this new feature. If Google can expand this feature to all Fast Pair-compatible devices, such as Bluetooth trackers, headphones, smartwatches, laptops, and more, it’ll be easier for consumers to keep tabs on their ever-growing armory of devices.

#3081. Apple Says There Are Now 100,000 Apps Built for the iPhone Duo

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The new 4:3 inner screen doesn't play nicely with every app, but here's some that work so far.

Apple’s first foldable, the iPhone Duo, may have come last to the foldable party, as CNET’s review noted, but it’s certainly not least. Senior reporter Abrar Al-Heeti praised it for the iOS 27 integration across both displays and its impressive durability, which has crossed 300,000 folds in our test and counting.

However, as the Duo has filtered down to the general public, some new owners have noted that some of their favorite apps aren’t well optimized for the larger 4:3 inner display. I noticed this myself when I opened the iPhone Duo and launched Discord, finding the app centered on the screen with black space on both sides, rather than expanding to fill the panel.

That’s not unusual for a phone with a brand-new hardware design, and we’ve seen it on many Android foldables, with optimized apps taking a little bit of time to roll out. Apple has confirmed that this is already changing, with over 100,000 apps built for the iPhone Duo to more fully take advantage of the 4:3 main screen.

“Today, there are well over 100,000 apps built for iPhone Duo already live on the App Store, and thousands of optimized apps are being added to the store each day,” said Susan Prescott, Apple’s vice president of Worldwide Developer Relations in a comment sent to CNET.

“In the last month alone, thousands of developers have joined us for workshops in 19 cities around the world to experience iPhone Duo firsthand and bring updated apps to their users,” Prescott said. “The enthusiasm has been incredible, and we’re excited to see the pace of updates continue to accelerate as more and more apps optimize for iPhone Duo.”

Among the apps, Microsoft Word and Outlook have been adjusted to look similar to their desktop versions, while Zoom uses the inner display as a bigger video conference room. Additionally, the company has confirmed that sports fans can use the NBA and NHL apps to follow live games and highlights on the larger screen.

Etsy and LTK Creator will also show more products in the feed, and productivity apps like Goodnotes and Extra will make more use of the additional screen real estate. There’s also Halide Mark III, a pro camera app that adapts to every pose, from a viewfinder on the cover screen when closed to a waist-level viewfinder with one tap when half open.

The iOS 27.2 update should also add support for Apple Pencil (USB-C), letting Duo users in various compatible apps, like Apple Notes, work directly on documents.

Apple shared a list of apps that have been recently optimized for the iPhone Duo, breaking them down into categories like Work, AI, Finances, News and more.

This isn’t an exclusive list by any means. CNET previously confirmed seven iPhone Duo-ready apps that make the most of Apple’s foldable screen: Netflix, NYT Cooking, Fantastical, Edits by Instagram, TikTok, Instagram and Toastie.

Apple fans have put together their own “does it work on iPhone Duo?” web tracker, giving you a sense of whether an app is fully optimized, partially fits or is still awaiting updates. I can confirm that Discord is currently listed as “partially fits,” meaning it uses most of the inner screen but not all of it, which tracks with my own experience with the letterboxing.

We expect that this is only the beginning, and we’ll see the list above expand in the coming weeks as updated apps are submitted to the App Store. Going forward, you should also see spotlights for iPhone Duo apps in categories like “Great Apps and Games” and “Popular Apps.” Apple is making it easier to identify apps compatible with the iPhone Duo by adding a Made for iPhone Duo badge to product pages.

#3080. Pet Insurance on Your Mind? Buy It Now, Not Later

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

If your pet is young and healthy, the cost of coverage may not seem worth it. But that math can change fast.

Senior Writer & Licensed Insurance Specialist

It feels natural to protect your pet’s health with insurance in the same way you protect your loved ones with health insurance. After all, fur babies are part of the family.

But if you’ve shopped for pet insurance recently, you might have cringed at the cost. Should you really pay hundreds of dollars every year for something you might not use? For some pet parents, the answer is yes, even if their pet is young and healthy. And that’s partly because of how pet insurance handles pre-existing conditions.

A pre-existing condition is any illness or injury that shows symptoms before your pet insurance policy takes effect. Pet insurers usually won’t cover these conditions.

Dr. Rebecca Greenstein, chief veterinarian at Kleinburg Veterinary Hospital and veterinary expert for rover.com, says she’s seeing an increase in claim denials based on pre-existing conditions. “The whole point of insurance is you want broad spectrum coverage for a variety of things,” she says. “By the time they're excluding major organ systems and anything related to that, you may be paying monthly for coverage that doesn't actually help you when you need to go make a claim.”

If you’re considering pet insurance, it’s best to buy a policy before your pet needs it.

Your pet’s pre-existing conditions will determine which treatments and procedures insurers cover down the road.

Say your dog has a history of allergies. If you buy a policy after your four-footed friend has shown symptoms, pet insurers typically won’t pay for allergy shots or other treatments. The exclusion might also apply to related problems like itchiness or ear infections, says Dr. Michael Goldstein, medical director at Billerica Veterinary Hospital and Thrive Pet Healthcare.

He offers the example of a dog who goes to the vet for a hotspot one year and an ear infection the next. If you got pet insurance for your dog between those visits, the insurer might deny your claim because both issues could be caused by underlying allergies.

The best way around this type of scenario is to buy coverage when your pet is young, before any symptoms develop.

» MORE: The best pet insurance for pre-existing conditions

It’s tricky to figure out if pet insurance will actually save you money in the long run. The most common type of plan covers accidents and illness. It costs an average of $836 for dogs and $435 for cats each year, according to the North American Pet Health Insurance Association (NAPHIA). You could pay these premiums for years before an emergency surgery or chronic illness makes its way to your financial doorstep.

It’s impossible to know for sure whether your pet will stay healthy or run up sky-high vet bills. But you can assess the potential value of pet insurance by looking at common chronic conditions that pets develop with age and how much it costs to treat them.

For instance, “cats generally, as younger animals, don't have a lot of problems,” Goldstein says. “But as they get older, there are definitely common issues. Older cats in particular get chronic kidney disease.”

In cats over age 10, the Cornell Feline Health Center estimates that as many as 40% have chronic kidney disease. The annual cost to treat this condition can top $1,200.

Another example is atopic dermatitis from skin allergies, a common ailment affecting as many as 15% of dogs, according to the Cornell Richard P. Riney Canine Health Center. A single vet visit to address these itchy, persistent symptoms can cost $250 or more. And while that’s a fraction of what you might pay for pet insurance, costs of chronic conditions can add up over your pet’s lifetime.

Vet bills can also balloon quickly if chronic conditions require surgery. One example of this is hip dysplasia, a genetic disorder that can cause arthritis and pain in large-breed dogs. Depending on the procedure, you could pay up to $10,000 for hip surgery.

Many pet insurance plans cover treatments for hip dysplasia, including surgical costs, as long as it's not a pre-existing condition. However, some policies have waiting periods of six to 12 months for orthopedic issues like hip dysplasia. If symptoms develop during that waiting period, the condition will be considered pre-existing. That’s another argument for buying your pet’s coverage earlier rather than later.

Most importantly, having insurance means you can choose the best treatment for your pet no matter the cost. “It's really tragic when it's a life and death decision that is needed and it’s based on someone's financial constraints,” Greenstein says. “What is best and what's affordable often are not on the same side of the spectrum.”

One option to keep pet insurance affordable is to buy an accident-only plan. This type of coverage is usually much cheaper, costing about $190 annually for dogs and $112 for cats, according to NAPHIA. These plans cover injuries resulting from accidents, which include not just wounds and broken bones but also objects or toxins your pet might swallow.

However, an accident-only plan won’t help with some of the most common vet expenses. Illnesses, not injuries, are behind most claims submitted to pet insurer Lemonade, the company said in an email. In the past year, more than 60% of Lemonade's pet insurance claims stemmed from illnesses. That’s twice as many as the next-largest claim category, which was preventive care. A similar trend can be found in data from Nationwide, where chronic diseases make up a clear majority of pet insurance claims submitted to the company in 2025.

If you opt for an accident-only plan or no coverage at all, it’s still smart to set aside some savings for those inevitable pet emergencies and illnesses. This pool of funds can keep you from having to make difficult decisions about your pet’s care based solely on the cost of treatment.
