#3079. Alpine Linux and OpenSSH patch flaw that’s as bad as it can get

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

This dangerous Linux security hole must be patched as soon as possible.

The perfect nightmare scenario for a Linux server is when all an attacker needs to do is reach the server’s Secure Shell (SSH) port, waltz right in, and take it over. That’s what happened to Alpine Linux, the popular, lightweight Linux server. Adding insult to injury, this happened only days after Alpine Linux dodged a security hole-sized bullet to its GitLab implementation.

Also: Linux Mint 27 arrives next month, and I like it already: What to expect

How bad is it? As bad as it can get. Alpine Linux explained in its security advisory that if an attacker can reach a vulnerable server’s SSH port, they can get in “with one connection,” and the attack “works every time, and needs no retries.”

As security engineer Gregory Bryant explained in his LinkedIn post:

Most authentication bypasses come from complex logic chains, but yesterday’s Alpine Linux OpenSSH vulnerability (CVE-2026-123158) comes down to a single character: *.”

If you run Alpine Linux (or another musl-based distro) with the default openssh-server package (no Pluggable Authentication Modules (PAM)) and PasswordAuthentication enabled, an attacker can log into password-disabled, or key-only accounts simply by sending a password between 257 and 1024 bytes long.

Alpine Linux isn’t the only operating system that uses musl, a lightweight version of the standard C library (libc). Other operating systems that use musl and are potentially vulnerable include Adélie Linux; Void Linux; Sabotage Linux; OpenWrt, an Android variant,  and the Yocto Project, which is used to build embedded Linux distributions.

Also: This clever banking scam keeps phishing by faking its own demise

Wind River, which produces the commercial embedded Linux distro, Wind River Linux, is also looking into the issue. A company representative told me, “Wind River takes security seriously. We are actively reviewing the reported vulnerability and assessing its potential impact. We will take appropriate action as needed.”

Behind the scenes, when OpenSSH is built without PAM, it verifies passwords by hashing the password attempt using the C library’s crypt() function. It then compares the result to the account’s stored password field. So far, so good.

But when a key is longer than 256 bytes, musl’s crypt() function returns the string * as an error indicator. That is also what the password field holds for any account with no password, such as a key-only account. Thus, the “hash” matches and the login succeeds, and the attacker walks in.

Also: The new Cutefish Linux distro might be the best MacOS clone around

To exploit it, an attacker only needs the name of such an account. The attacker also needs at least one other account on the machine with a 5 or 6 password hash, and then to send a password of 257 to 1,024 bytes. According to Alpine, one user with a password is enough to make every passwordless account accessible.

In Linux systems that use glibc’s crypt(), which is almost all of them, the same attempt would return NULL on error, which sshd already rejects, so Debian, Ubuntu, Fedora, etc., aren’t affected.

According to *sshole, the group that uncovered the problem, this is a side effect that has been hiding since 2016.

Alpine said every release it checked is affected: 3.21 through 3.24, edge, and the older 3.16 to 3.20 branches. Exposure requires the following:

Root is not directly exposed in most cases. It has shipped as root:* in the base image since about Alpine 3.16, but OpenSSH’s default PermitRootLogin prohibit-password blocks it. Another account that can become root, for example through doas, still gives an attacker a route to root.

Alpine singled out its official tiny-cloud images, versions 3.18.11 through 3.24.3, as especially vulnerable. These ship with an alpine user with a * password field and passwordless doas. They are safe on first boot but become exploitable once any 6 hash exists on the machine. That can happen when an administrator sets a console password or when user data adds a user with a hashed password, which means, essentially, all of them.

Alpine released 3.21.10, 3.22.8, 3.23.8 and 3.24.4 on Thursday, with the OpenSSH fix among the security changes. It also published fixed packages for each release back to 3.16. Alpine 3.21 reached end of support on Nov. 1.

The just-released OpenSSH 10.6p2 avoids the problem in sshd. Alpine’s stable branches carry a patch for the same problem. You should run apk upgrade -U -a and then restart the daemon with rc-service sshd restart. Alpine stressed that upgrading the package does not replace the running daemon. Anyone who cannot update should set PasswordAuthentication no and restart sshd, after confirming that key login works.

Also: Maze Linux is the best security-focused distro I’ve ever tested

To check for an attack, Alpine told administrators to search /var/log/messages for “Accepted password for” lines that name an account with no password. It also advised reviewing dedicated OpenBSD application subexecutor (doas) use and recently created users, files, and authorized keys. “If you cannot rule out a login, treat the machine as compromised.” Just what you wanted for your weekend, I know, but here we are.

#3078. Here's What a $1,000 Investment in Tesla Stock Could Be Worth in 5 Years

Recognized as a market-beating portfolio addition over the long term, Tesla shares have actually fallen 16% in the past half decade.

Investors view Tesla (TSLA +4.23%) as a perennial winner. Over the last decade, this is true. Shares have soared 2,630% since early November 2016 (as of Nov. 5, 2026).

But the story takes a dramatic turn if you zoom in. This "Magnificent Seven" stock has actually dropped 16% in the past five years. That performance means that it has significantly underperformed the broader market. Investors are left wondering if the company's shares are staying out of the fast lane permanently.

Here's what a $1,000 investment in Tesla could be worth in five years. There is, without a doubt, significant upside. However, uncertainty is sky-high.

One of the core tenets of Warren Buffett's investing philosophy is to own what you know. Investors must be able to confidently figure out what a business will look like in the future. Naturally, this limits the opportunity set to stable and predictable companies.

Tesla is the opposite. It's like the business is always undergoing some sort of strategic restructuring. This makes it extremely difficult to assess what the future will bring.

Five years from now, Tesla's operations could look very similar to what they are today. During the third quarter (ended Sept. 30), 73% of the company's revenue came from selling electric vehicles (EVs). This business continues to struggle, though, as competition diverts consumer spending to rival offerings, which has forced Tesla to lower pricing. Deliveries dipped 2% year over year in Q3.

It also doesn't help that car demand is exposed to changing economic winds, adding volatility to the mix. Just ask the legacy mass-market auto manufacturers how much their profits can fluctuate based on interest rates and other macro variables.

Tesla is working on exciting projects that attract the market's attention. While the Robotaxi service is only running in select markets right now, with Cybercab only in Austin, Texas, the future of the company rests on autonomous vehicle technology. The ultimate goal is to run a global fleet that collects recurring revenue at tremendous scale.

Optimus, the humanoid robot, is another important part of the story. These won't be available for sale until the end of 2027, at the earliest. However, Elon Musk said in 2024 that this product could drive Tesla's market capitalization to $25 trillion one day.

Tesla's future rests squarely on its ability to bring artificial intelligence (AI) capabilities into the physical world. Self-driving EVs and robotics are the avenues. Their market opportunities appear to be massive. But there are an unlimited number of questions surrounding technical advancements, scaling production, regulatory developments, user adoption, safety and reliability, and timelines.

If you view them at a high level, it's easy to assume that shares are egregiously expensive. The belief is that this has always been the case. Investors can buy Tesla stock right now by paying a forward price-to-earnings ratio of 151.

That multiple is more than 11 times higher than what Nvidia trades at. The dominant artificial intelligence infrastructure business is forecast to bring in $190 billion of free cash flow in fiscal 2027. That's almost double what Tesla will report as revenue this year.

The Elon Musk premium is real. The investment community prizes what he says and does. And the general view is that he's the greatest entrepreneur and visionary ever.

This feeds into the positive sentiment. Tesla's strongest bulls will argue that the company's optionality signals that shares are severely undervalued. Robotaxi and Optimus will usher in a new era of financial prosperity that will contribute to unprecedented profit growth.

The bears point to a track record of over-promising and under-delivering on key milestones and deadlines. It seems that Tesla has always been working toward some ambitious goal that it never fully achieves. And investors are always left having to buy the narrative and ignore the current reality of the business.

An extreme valuation coupled with sky-high uncertainty lead me to believe that the stock will largely stay flat over the next five years, leaving investors with the $1,000 that they started with.

#3077. iPhone Duo vs. Galaxy Z Fold 8 Camera Comparison: Frolicking With Apple’s and Samsung’s Foldables

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

I snapped some photos in a dreamy garden with these passport-size phones to see how their rear cameras stack up.

There are a handful of things Apple’s iPhone Duo and Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 8 have in common. They both feature a passport-size design, two rear cameras and roughly the same battery capacity. They’re also two of my favorite phones I’ve reviewed this year.

The compact, book-style configuration feels like the future of foldables, and Apple and Samsung have each nailed their offerings. Both phones are incredibly versatile, giving you the option to quickly swipe through apps on the palm-sized cover display or open up to a more sprawling 7.6-inch inner screen that’s great for watching videos and multitasking. That innovation comes at a cost, though, with the Duo starting at $1,999 for 256GB and the Z Fold 8 starting at $1,900 for the same storage level.

There are some drawbacks to having a more compact design, namely when it comes to the rear cameras. There’s no telephoto camera on either phone, which can make the high price tags a little harder to swallow. Plus, the wide and ultra-wide cameras aren’t quite as capable as what you’ll get on premium non-folding phones such as the iPhone 18 Pro and Galaxy S26 Ultra, which have more advanced optical zoom capabilities and larger main-camera sensors.

Still, the iPhone Duo’s and Z Fold 8’s cameras are nothing to scoff at. The Duo has a 48-megapixel wide and 48-megapixel ultrawide camera, as well as a 12-megapixel selfie camera on the cover screen and a FaceTime camera on the inner display (Apple hasn’t shared the resolution). Meanwhile, the Z Fold 8 has a 50-megapixel wide and 50-megapixel ultrawide camera, plus 10-megapixel selfie cameras on the cover and inner displays. That means you should still get photos that impress, whether you’re in the brightly lit outdoors or in a dark, moody setting.

To compare the cameras, I took the iPhone Duo and Galaxy Z Fold 8 to Filoli, a historic house and garden in the San Francisco Bay Area. These are some of my favorite shots, highlighting the strengths and unique characteristics of each phone’s cameras.

Both phones do a great job depicting the vibrancy of the lush background, but the iPhone Duo has the edge here. It better preserves the detail in the stone bench and flooring, and the greenery takes on a deeper, more satisfying hue.

These photos turned out very similarly, and both are equally dazzling. But the Z Fold 8 image is slightly sharper, as seen in the lines on the tree bark and the brighter, more pigmented greenery and flora. So I’m giving this one to the Z Fold 8.

These portrait images highlight some of the key differences in color and tone between the iPhone Duo and Galaxy Z Fold 8. I prefer the richer blacks and more vibrant colors captured by the iPhone Duo. It also does a better job of keeping the raven in focus, allowing the bird to stand out more distinctly against the background and creating a more striking overall image. By comparison, the Z Fold 8 produces a softer, gentler look, with a more subdued overall tone.

I like the brightness of the iPhone Duo image, and while the Z Fold 8 shot has slightly deeper shadows, the hydrangeas are also more vibrant. The blues and greens pop more on the Z Fold 8, as does the sky, which is a little washed out on the Duo. So this one goes to the Z Fold 8.

I keep flipping on this one, but ultimately, the richer colors and texture on the Duo image have won me over. The iPhone’s camera tends to cast a warmer glow, as you can see in the stone benches, while the Z Fold 8 takes on a more neutral tone. In the end, the differences are subtle and a matter of personal preference.

And finally, here’s me unwinding with a book. By default, the iPhone Duo applies a pleasing background blur, which I appreciate for the added dramatic effect. (This effect can be disabled when taking a photo, but I rarely choose to do so.) The Z Fold 8, by comparison, keeps the distant lawns and forested hills in sharper focus, preserving those details remarkably well. Ultimately, I prefer the Duo’s approach, which keeps the book in sharper focus and makes it stand out from the background. That said, I do appreciate the Z Fold 8’s ability to capture finer details throughout the scene, particularly the crisp individual blades of grass.

Because I’m a fan of both the iPhone Duo and the Galaxy Z Fold 8, it’s difficult to wholeheartedly recommend one camera over the other. Each has its strengths, and there are moments where one preserves detail and color better than the other.

In general, photos from the iPhone Duo tend to have warmer tones and richer, deeper colors, which can also lead to more pronounced shadows. Meanwhile, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 often produces brighter images, though this can sometimes come at the expense of finer details. But these results can vary depending on factors such as lighting and angles.

Ultimately, both foldable phones deliver impressive results, and the choice comes down to which image characteristics you value most. Thankfully, you really can’t go wrong with either.

#3076. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

You’ll also get an in-stay credit when you book a vacation home through The Edit for four nights or more.

Starting in 2027, Chase’s The Edit collection of upscale hotel and resort stays will broaden to include more than 10,000 vacation homes. If you carry the Chase Sapphire Reserve® or the Sapphire Reserve for Business℠, that means you’ll soon have more ways to use The Edit credits on those cards.

This addition will significantly expand The Edit’s footprint — currently at more than 1,500 hotels and resorts — although its usefulness will depend on your travel style. The new properties are curated in partnership with Plum Guide, a vacation-rental booking platform.

Plus, when you book a stay of four nights or more through The Edit, you’ll get a $150 in-stay credit to use at your vacation home.

Here’s everything you need to know about the upcoming changes to The Edit.

Eligible Chase cardholders can currently book vacation homes through the issuer’s travel portal. However, those bookings are not eligible to receive The Edit benefits. That changes Jan. 1, 2027, with select listings.

If you have the Chase Sapphire Reserve® or the Sapphire Reserve for Business℠, here’s what you can expect when you book a vacation home through The Edit:

Up to $250 in statement credits on prepaid stays of two nights or more. Chase Sapphire Reserve® cardholders are capped at $500 in credits a year, while Sapphire Reserve for Business℠ cardholders get up to $1,000 (starting Jan. 1, 2027).

$150 in-stay credit when booking a stay of at least four nights.

Early check-in and late checkout, when available.

Compared to booking hotels and resorts with The Edit, you’re losing out on perks like a $100 property credit (which doesn’t have a four-night minimum), complimentary breakfast and room upgrades.

While cardholders will be able to redeem points toward vacation-home stays, Chase told NerdWallet that these bookings will not be eligible for Points Boost at launch.

You’ll also need to book vacation homes at least seven days in advance — a requirement that’s currently in place with standard vacation homes available in Chase’s travel portal. Chase said it plans to reduce that requirement in the near future.

Chase’s addition of vacation homes to The Edit follows American Express’ recent move to add Airbnb Luxe properties to its own lodging credit for eligible cardholders.

When you book a vacation home for four nights or more through The Edit, you’ll get a $150 in-stay credit to use on services like massages, a private chef, dry cleaning and airport pick-ups.

Options vary by property, but you’ll see a list of available offerings prior to booking your stay. You won’t be able to see pricing, but you can reach out to a Reserve travel designer — a travel concierge who can help plan your trip — to get pricing estimates prior to booking. (You won’t be able to contact the vacation home host until after you’ve booked your stay.)

While it’s nice to get the credit, the four-night minimum could be frustrating for cardholders. When you book hotels through The Edit, there isn’t a stay minimum to get the $100 property credit (although you must stay two nights to use your eligible card’s $250 prepaid stay credit).

By comparison, AmEx offers a $250 Airbnb Experiences credit on the American Express Platinum Card® or The Business Platinum Card® from American Express each calendar year when you book a prepaid Airbnb Luxe property through its travel portal. Terms apply. That statement credit is good for the rest of the calendar year in which the booking is confirmed. However, you can only unlock one $250 credit a year, even if you make multiple bookings.

Booking a vacation home through The Edit makes the most sense for longer group trips.

One of the best perks of booking a vacation home is that everyone gets to stay together in a shared space — something that’s difficult to do in a traditional hotel. If you’re able to get a larger group, you’ll also have more people to split the booking cost with.

Plus, you’ll want to stay at least four nights to benefit from the $150 in-stay credit.

If you’re traveling by yourself or with a partner on a shorter trip, you might find more value staying at a hotel or resort that’s part of The Edit. You’ll get useful perks like complimentary breakfast for two and a $100 property credit.

Ultimately, the biggest benefit of adding vacation homes to The Edit is creating more booking options. If vacation homes fit your travel style, this is a great development to look forward to. If not, you’ll still be able to book hotels through The Edit like before.

No annual fee: Wells Fargo Autograph® Card

Flat-rate travel rewards: Capital One Venture Rewards Credit Card

#3073. Wave: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

WVE-007 preserves muscle while cutting fat, a therapeutic edge that could reshape how obesity treatments are designed.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Wave (WVE +4.50%) announced advancing clinical momentum across its RNA (ribonucleic acid) treatment portfolio. The biotechnology business is validating its drug delivery platform through human trial data while securing streamlined regulatory pathways. This combination of platform proof and multiyear cash coverage reduces financial risk for shareholders as major clinical readouts approach.

Wave is advancing WVE-007, an siRNA (small interfering RNA, a technology that silences specific disease-causing genes) candidate targeting obesity by promoting fat loss while preserving lean tissue. Weight-loss treatments like GLP-1s (glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonists) make patients shed muscle alongside fat, but Wave's therapy doesn't. That distinction creates opportunities across monotherapy, combination care, and post-treatment maintenance.

Following discussions with the FDA (U.S. Food and Drug Administration), Wave aligned on a single two-year registrational trial for WVE-006 in AATD (alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency, a genetic condition causing lung and liver damage). The trial includes a one-year interim analysis that could support accelerated regulatory approval without requiring a separate confirmatory study.

Collaboration partner GSK (GlaxoSmithKline) selected a development candidate from Wave's platform, triggering a $12 million milestone payment to the company. That milestone adds to a substantial cash position that funds business operations well into 2028.

Clinical trials are advancing on schedule, and management isn't hiding its optimism. Proprietary RNA chemistry gives Wave a structural advantage in gene targeting, while GSK milestone payments validate the platform. Management is watching clinical readouts across its obesity and liver disease programs, focusing on execution.

Long-term value depends on converting platform capabilities into commercial therapies. Wave continues using human genetics data to lower trial risks across its portfolio.

#3072. Wendy’s Just Released a Video Game Where You Can Win Fast Food

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

I won a medium fries by defeating a sinister robot that looked suspiciously similar to a McDonald’s french fry container.

Wendy’s has turned its red-headed mascot into a wisecracking warrior who battles evil french fry robots in a free mobile game called WendyQuest. The company announced the retro-style role-playing game on Nov. 4. The announcement came with a promotion where you can win codes in the game, which are redeemable for menu items like fries and burgers at Wendy’s locations.

WendyQuest is available to download on iOS and Android. Desktop users can get it through the Steam platform. Anyone can play the game, but the promotion is restricted to people who are 18 and up living in the US.

You need to sign up for a Wendy’s account to redeem a code. While the food you can win is free, it requires you to buy something when you go.

“Wendy’s has been a part of gaming culture for years, including on Twitch, Fortnite and Feast of Legends, but WendyQuest is the brand’s first fully owned video game,” a spokesperson for Wendy’s said.

In 2019, Wendy’s partnered with the online multiplayer video game Fortnite, as well as the video-streaming platform Twitch, for various promotions featuring the company’s mascot. The company even released a rule book for a traditional tabletop roleplaying game the same year, called Feast of Legends, as a free download.

I downloaded WendyQuest, and my daughter and I played it for a while. The blocky graphics are reminiscent of the home-console video games of the late 1980s and early 1990s. It plays a lot like a simplified version of the DragonQuest, Final Fantasy and Breath of Fire games from the era, only stuffed with fast-food branding.

Rather than the regular turn-based battles that come standard in the early RPGs, this game adds a timing element, where you need to press a button at the right time like you do in old golfing video games.

It’s not a bad game. The light humor is suitable for younger players, and it’s fun to play. Like many RPGs, you battle monsters to gain levels, search around the maps for hidden areas, buy items and talk to people in towns to figure out what’s going on.

It took us over an hour to complete the first region, Fryshire, and get the Fry Stone, which unlocks a promo code for medium fries, free with a purchase. But again, you have to sign up for a Wendy’s account on their website or on the company’s mobile app.

Video games have a long history of offering real-world prizes to players as promotions. Swordquest, a series of video games released in the early 1980s for the Atari 2600, famously held a contest where players tried to win actual treasure made of gold and jewels.

In 2015, a promotion for the video game Just Cause 3 offered players the chance to win a private island. Developer Square Enix clarified that it couldn’t guarantee that the island would be livable and that the winner would have to pay all the taxes involved.

The reward codes from WendyQuest expire seven days after you get them, and Wendy’s says the codes are first come, first served.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Rates fell today, but not by enough to change your mortgage math.

Mortgage rates fell today, possibly helped by post-midterm election clarity and a drop in oil prices.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 7.01% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is six basis points lower than yesterday and 17 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Tomorrow, the Nerds will be watching the release of the October jobs report. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.51% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3070. Nvidia Board Launches Probe After Reports CEO Was Targeted by Chinese Spy

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Chipmaker's probe comes as the CEO admits to having an affair with the suspected spy.

Nvidia on Sunday said it’s opening an investigation into whether Jensen Huang, the company’s chief executive officer and founder, was targeted by a foreign intelligence effort after Huang admitted having an affair with a business executive who was reportedly a Chinese spy.

The announcement of any investigation follows a series of reports that Huang had developed a “close relationship” with Alice Huang, no relation to Jensen Huang, who is a Chinese citizen and an executive at Megaspeed. Megaspeed is a Singapore-based data center company that buys chips from Nvidia to run data centers that sell computing power to Chinese AI labs.

The New York Times reported in late October that US intelligence agencies had Alice Huang under electronic surveillance for allegedly facilitating the sale of advanced Nvidia chips to China in violation of US law. During that surveillance, officials discovered she was also allegedly funneling information to the Chinese government in Beijing.

The announcement comes as Nvidia has solidified its position as a leader in the AI boom. The cutting-edge computer chips that Nvidia produces are what make AI and AI data centers possible, and that has made Nvidia one of the world’s most valuable companies by market cap.

“Today’s news will not slow Nvidia,” an Nvidia spokesperson said. “Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption.”

A committee of independent directors, working with outside counsel, will oversee the investigation, the board said in a statement. The company added that it “has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board’s inquiry will examine all available information.”

The US has tried to control China’s access to these advanced chips for years. The Biden administration banned Chinese sales of a series of advanced Nvidia chips in 2022, but President Donald Trump relaxed those rules in 2025.  But reports show that there are still illegal operations underway to smuggle banned chips from the US to China.

In his own memo to Nvidia employees, Huang said he was “deeply sorry” for the affair. “I was unfaithful to my wife,” he wrote. “My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did.” Huang, 63, added that he will cooperate fully with the board’s investigation.

The company had previously characterized Jensen Huang’s interactions with Alice Huang as “not unusual or unexpected” given that the CEO “talks with thousands of customer executives every year.”

CNET’s Katelyn Chedraoui contributed to this report.

#3069. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3068. What Does a ‘Blue Wave’ Mean for Mortgage Rates?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Democrats won big in the midterms, but mortgage rates have bigger forces to reckon with.

Mortgage rates fell again this week, extending a gradual slide that began well before Election Day.

The average rate on a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage fell 13 basis points to 7.05% APR in the week ending Nov. 5, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.) We calculate our weekly average using daily APRs recorded over the past five business days.

Homebuyers’ budgets are feeling the pinch from high mortgage rates, steep home prices and a shortage of listings. On the campaign trail, candidates from both parties promised to make homeownership more affordable. But solving that problem is like untangling 20 years’ worth of Christmas lights from your attic. Yank on one strand, and three more tighten.

“Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run and more housing supply plus a more dynamic labor market in the long run,” Jake Krimmel, senior economist for Realtor.com, said in an email.

A shift in political power won’t erase those challenges overnight. Here's what's driving mortgage rates right now — and where a Democratic Congress might have some pull.

How that affects mortgage rates: The relief of one less uncertainty is giving rates some breathing room.

Election results have cleared up one big unknown for investors. With Democrats controlling Congress and Republicans holding the White House, markets are bracing for the reality that any major spending bills could face roadblocks.

Political gridlock isn't always popular, but weirdly, investors sometimes welcome it. When a divided government limits new spending, the government may need to borrow less than expected — which could turn down the heat on mortgage rates.

But there’s a catch. That same gridlock could make it harder to pass laws to help bring down housing costs.

How that affects mortgage rates: Heavy borrowing from the government and tech companies adds upward pressure to the bond market, where mortgage rates take their cues.

When the government spends more than it collects in taxes, it borrows the difference by selling Treasury bonds. When there's lots of debt for sale, investors may demand higher returns, called yields. Mortgage rates tend to follow the yield on the 10-year Treasury note.

The new Congress still faces a mountain of government debt. What they choose to do about it can ripple through the economy to affect your monthly mortgage payment.

“Sound fiscal policy, rather than deficit-financed spending and standoffs over budgets and the debt limit, can help keep rates in check,” Andy Winkler, managing director for housing and infrastructure policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, said in an email.

Meanwhile, Big Tech companies are borrowing a boatload of money to build AI data centers. To get the cash, they issue corporate bonds, giving investors another place to put their money. More bonds flooding the market can push Treasury yields higher, taking mortgage rates along for the ride.

But how long can the AI building boom last? Voters across party lines pushed back against data centers in the midterms. Even if lawmakers pump the brakes on new construction, a slowdown in tech spending doesn't guarantee cheaper mortgages.

How that affects mortgage rates: Wartime disruptions to global oil supplies can fuel inflation, pushing borrowing costs higher.

The unpopular war in Iran likely added to the voter frustration that helped fuel Democrats' midterm victories. Higher energy costs can hit household budgets in multiple ways: at the pump, through pricier goods that need to be shipped, and through higher interest rates on loans (including mortgages) as inflation heats up.

“A resolution would likely improve oil supply, helping to ease price pressures and potentially providing relief for mortgage rates,” Krimmel said.

A Democratic Congress could push for a different approach to ending the war, but it can't make Iran agree to a deal.

Meanwhile, stubborn inflation from the Iran war has raised the prospect of another Federal Reserve rate hike at its Dec. 8-9 meeting. Futures traders now put the odds of a quarter-point increase at more than 80%, according to CME FedWatch. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but investors anticipate its decisions — so mortgage rates often move before the Fed does.

If buying a home makes sense for your budget now, there's little reason to wait just because Congress changed hands. Buyers can sometimes score lower prices in winter, when sellers are more willing to negotiate. But with today’s mortgage rates hovering above 7%, higher borrowing costs can eat into those savings, so it’s important to compare mortgage lenders to get the best rate.

For longer-term hope, look to the bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which became law this summer. This legislation shows lawmakers can, in fact, work together to make meaningful progress on housing affordability.

“Even with divided government, lawmakers and the administration can build on that common ground to further boost housing production, preserve existing affordable homes, and help families struggling with high housing and utility costs,” Winkler said.

But new homes take time to build, and your house hunt doesn't have to follow Congress's calendar. Your homebuying budget and local market conditions matter more than the next political headline.

#3067. Hubbell: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Factory bottlenecks are easing, but the real test is whether pricing power can hold as capacity catches up to demand.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Hubbell (HUBB +2.54%) announced double-digit sales growth and raised its full-year earnings guidance. The company is expanding its factory footprint to catch up with massive demand across electrical power grids and data centers. High demand combined with pricing discipline protects profit margins against persistent material cost inflation.

Sales of components for data center electrical systems grew rapidly because cloud infrastructure expansion feeds directly into core equipment orders. Expanding factory equipment allowed the company to keep pace with customer orders.

Utility customers are ordering power grid hardware faster than factories ship it, creating a growing backlog. Aging electrical grid upgrades and new power connections for artificial intelligence facilities provide multiyear growth visibility.

To overcome factory bottlenecks in high-demand product lines, management is adding physical plant space and new machinery. Doubling quarterly capacity investments helps unlock revenue that was previously constrained by factory limits.

Order books are filling faster than usual as electric grid modernization and data center builds accelerate across North America. Specified product positioning and an extensive installed base provide strong pricing power against raw material inflation.

Management is expanding factory floor space to clear delivery bottlenecks while maintaining a high return on invested capital. Electric power transmission and distribution (T&D) infrastructure investments provide a long tailwind for multiyear growth.

#3065. NRC Health: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

That expansion signals steady customer demand for experience management tools even as implementation costs compress near-term profits.

Management held adjusted EBITDA, or earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, margins steady at 27% while funding implementation capacity for major health system contracts. Upfront investments in delivery teams will weigh on short-term profits before contract expansion fuels revenue in the second half of the year.

A vast majority of the company's healthcare clients buy only one solution line, leaving substantial room for contract expansion. Execution hinges on converting recent contract wins into profitable recurring revenue while deploying cash prudently across internal research, strategic M&A, or mergers and acquisitions, and share buybacks.

#3064. Marriott Bonvoy Boundless vs. Bevy: Which One Comes Out On Top?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

The right card for you may depend on where you travel, how often, and your level of elite status with Bonvoy.

Two of the popular credit cards in the Marriott Bonvoy portfolio deliver various benefits to their users. The right one for you may depend on your typical travel patterns and history, such as if you already have a high level of elite status with Marriott or you travel only to certain regions.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card and the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card both come with valuable perks, but there are quite a few considerations that are worth a thorough review. Many travelers prefer not to have two cards from the same loyalty program, and these two cards are actually quite different from each other (including being issued by different banks).

Let’s take a look at these two Marriott co-branded cards to see if one may serve your needs over the other.

Here's how the cards stack up on the basics.

Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card

While the “winning” card for some may not apply to everyone, we wanted to analyze the card that provides exceptional benefits for travelers looking for a Marriott card in exchange for the cost of the annual fee. Those new to Marriott will find this to be a solid entry-level card, but it also works for Marriott Bonvoy fans looking for some extra perks without a huge outlay of money for an annual fee. These are the benefits that handed this card the win.

The annual fee on the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card is notably less at $95 compared with $250 on the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card. While the earning rates when using the card to pay for Marriott stays are nearly the same, and both have bonus categories where you can earn multiples of points based on everyday spending, the lower-annual-fee card offers a lot of points earning for its low annual fee.

On top of the lower annual fee, it comes with an automatic annual free night award, Silver Elite status and a fast path to achieve Gold if you use the card often. Add to that an annual $100 in airline credits and DoorDash DashPass membership, and you have more than covered the annual fee without considering the other perks.

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card card’s $250 annual fee is higher, and its annual free night award requires spending $15,000 in eligible purchases. But the card comes with automatic Gold Elite status and more bonus points earned.

There are other cards (like American Express Platinum Card®) that also include free Gold Elite status with Marriott that can provide decent value for their annual fee (enrollment required). From a pure cash maximization perspective, the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card wins because it provides more bang for your buck.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card is the only one of the two cards to include an annual free night award, and it has a lower annual fee. It can be used at properties up to 35,000 points a night. This can render great value and is more than worth the $95 annual fee. Hands down, it is the winner in this category.

If you spend more than $15,000 on the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card in a calendar year, you can unlock a free night certificate valid at up to 50,000 points. This is even more valuable than the included certificate that comes with the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card, but it also requires you to spend more on this card to get it.

It is important to consider the opportunity cost of not putting certain types of spend on other cards where you could be earning greater multiples of points for the same type of spending. Would losing those points be worth the value of a one-night-free certificate? Since Marriott relies on dynamic pricing, which means that points rates for a redemption night can fluctuate regularly, in many cases the value may not reach the level of the missed points opportunity.

While both cards offer decent travel protection benefits, only the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card comes with a flight credit. You can earn up to $100 in statement credits when making purchases directly with airlines by spending $250 on the card before the end of 2026 for a $50 statement credit and another $50 credit by spending $50 before June 30, 2027. That gives another point to the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card Boundless card.

» Learn more: Should I be loyal to a single airline?

If you don’t already have a Marriott co-branded credit card and you want to diversify what’s in your wallet, this could be a great option for big spenders. And if you are looking for one of the fastest paths to high Marriott elite status, this card can help you get there.

Both the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card and Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card cards offer bonus points on spending, but the more expensive annual fee Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card card offers more points on more spending opportunities, including the below:

18.5X points on Marriott Bonvoy property spending (1.5X more than the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card card).

4X points at global restaurants, including U.S. takeout and delivery plus U.S. Supermarkets on up to $15,000 in combined purchases, then 2X points (that’s 1X more than what Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card offers and available on $9,000 more in spending).

1,000 bonus points when staying at a Marriott Bonvoy property.

Depending on how much you spend in these categories, you have the ability to earn more points with this card than the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card, including when you are not traveling. And then when you are, you earn 1,000 Marriott bonus points for each paid, eligible stay booked directly with Marriott. This gives it an edge, even though the annual fee is higher.

» Learn more: Hilton versus Marriott — Which one is right for you?

When you get the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card, you’ll receive 15 elite night credits deposited into your Marriott account. This is on top of the instant Gold Elite status you earn with the card, which normally requires staying 25 nights. To reach the next tier, Platinum, you only need an additional 10 nights to reach its 50-night minimum.

This means not only do you start out at a higher elite tier with Marriott just for having this card, but also you can progress more quickly to Platinum. For frequent travelers, the best value of this card is the opportunity to reach the next status tier faster. And if you wanted to keep going, you would only need 35 more nights to get to Titanium, which is rich with on-property perks like lounge access, welcome gifts and room upgrades.

Earning these nights within a calendar year is not difficult for Marriott loyalists, especially if you hit the jackpot during a promotion that offers double elite nights.

The status perks don’t stop there. Once you hit Marriott’s Titanium status, you can then link your account with United Airlines to earn United’s Premier Silver elite status for more benefits when you fly United, including greater baggage allowance and free extra-legroom economy seats at check-in.

» Learn more: Marriott elite status: what you need to know

Both of these cards provide Marriott Bonvoy points earning and elite status, and they both offer a great way to expedite points earning or receive travel and purchase protections when using the card. But the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card packs a greater punch in its benefits for a lower annual fee. It becomes a great additional card to others you may have (like the American Express Platinum Card®, which has its own impressive travel perk lineup).

If you are a Marriott frequent guest, the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card offers higher-tier Gold status and a faster path to even better tiers for perks. But, it costs more each year and does not offer a free annual night.

Deciding which perks are more important for you, as well as looking at what benefits come from other cards in your wallet, will help you make an informed decision on which of the two best suits your needs.

For rates and fees of the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card, please visit American Express' rates and fees page.

#3063. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3062. Procter & Gamble's Dividend Has Survived 26 Recessions. Here's What $10,000 Now Earns in Yearly Dividend Income.

P&G has paid a dividend through 26 recessions and raised it for 70 straight years. Here's what $10,000 earns today, and why the streak looks safe.

Since Procter & Gamble (PG -1.02%) was incorporated in 1890, the National Bureau of Economic Research has counted 26 U.S. recessions. The Panic of 1893. The Great Depression. The 1970s, twice. The dot-com bust, the financial crisis, the pandemic. Through all of it, the company that makes Tide and Pampers mailed a dividend check every single quarter, and for the last 70 years each annual check has been larger than the one before. That makes this ticker a Dividend King, a designation for companies that have increased annual dividends for at least 50 consecutive years.

All of this is not a marketing line. It is the closest thing the stock market has to a promise kept. The question for anyone buying the stock today is whether a promise that old still has something behind it.

Procter & Gamble raised its quarterly dividend to $1.0885 per share in April, a 3% increase. That works out to $4.35 a year. With the stock trading near $166, the yield is about 2.6%, so a $10,000 investment buys roughly 60 shares and produces about $265 a year in dividend income.

That is not a number that changes anyone's life. What changes a life is what the number does over time. P&G's dividend has risen more than 60% over the past decade, and if the next 10 years look like the last 10, that $260-ish becomes something north of $430 a year on the same shares, without buying another one. The point of owning P&G is not the yield you get today. It is the yield you get on the price you paid, 10 and 20 years from now, from a company that has never once broken the streak.

A 136-year dividend history is really a statement about management discipline. Every Procter & Gamble CEO since Benjamin Harrison was U.S. president has viewed the dividend as the one thing they were not allowed to touch, which forces a certain conservatism in how the company borrows, acquires, and spends. P&G's own language in the April release says it plainly: many shareholders "rely on the steady, reliable income earned with their investment in P&G."

That matters now, because things are getting harder. On its October earnings call, P&G said it expects roughly $1.4 billion in higher commodity, energy, and freight costs this fiscal year, and that oil above $100 a barrel would be an "increasing headwind." Core gross margin fell 150 basis points in the quarter. This is the operating environment in which weaker consumer companies cut their payouts; Campbell's (CPB -1.28%) and Flowers Foods (FLO -5.49%) both did this year.

The encouraging part is that demand is holding. First-quarter organic sales rose 4%, with growth in all 10 product categories and all six regions,  most of it coming from volume rather than price. That is the healthiest kind of growth a consumer company can have, because it means people are buying more Tide and Crest, not just paying more for the same amount. New CEO Shailesh Jejurikar, who took over in January, also added Thorne, a supplements brand, pushing P&G into a wellness category it had never entered. The company maintained its full-year outlook despite the cost pressure, and the stock posted its best day in more than a year after the report.

The harder part is margin. P&G built its reputation on raising prices without losing customers, and that lever is nearly spent; pricing contributed almost nothing to the quarter. The company is leaning on productivity programs to absorb the cost squeeze, and management has been candid that fiscal 2027 "won't be a straight line."

To me, yes, and the reason is structural rather than sentimental. P&G pays out about two-thirds of its earnings as dividends, which leaves a cushion even in a bad year. It generates more than $15 billion in annual profit from products that people buy in a downturn, sometimes in smaller sizes, but rarely not at all.

A $10,000 stake earning $260 a year will not make you rich. But it has a better chance than almost any stock on the market of still paying you, in larger amounts, when the next recession comes and goes.

Micah Zimmerman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool recommends Campbell's. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3061. Could Buying Palantir Stock Today Set You Up for Life?

The company's strong leadership in AI software has propelled the stock to life-changing returns since late 2020.

Palantir Technologies (PLTR +3.48%) has already changed lives for investors who bought in early. Since the company's direct listing in 2020, shares have soared by more than 1,500%. That would have turned $10,000 into roughly $162,000 in roughly five years.

But can buying Palantir stock now still set you up for life? Unfortunately, math is not on your side. Despite the company's blistering growth, the stock's next 1,500% will probably take far longer. Here's why.

Few companies can match the explosive growth Palantir is achieving. It generated just over $2.3 billion in total revenue in the third quarter of 2026, growing 96% year over year and 20% over the prior quarter. Palantir has been posting eye-popping growth for a few years now, which is how the stock has produced such high returns.

That success has already made Palantir, at a market cap of $412 billion, one of the world's largest companies. It's a hefty valuation for a company of Palantir's revenue base, even with that rampant growth rate. At its current price, shares trade at 55 times sales, making it one of Wall Street's more expensive names.

But even if Palantir sustains that valuation, it's going to be difficult for the stock to continue rising as it has. Even another 100% will push it to near a $1 trillion market cap. There aren't many stocks worth $1 trillion or more, and those that are all generate significantly more revenue.

So it's unrealistic to expect Palantir to rise another 1,500% anytime soon. It will probably take years of prolonged success.

Of course, Palantir can still set you up for life with returns of 100% to 300%. The catch is that you need to invest a bit more than you would for a stock that grows 1,500%. That leads to the question of whether it's wise to risk an enormous capital investment in just one stock.

Such a large investment is difficult for most diversified portfolios. It's probably wiser to lower your expectations for Palantir stock than to take a big risk by putting too much of your portfolio into one company.

The artificial intelligence (AI) trade has eroded in the early sessions following the recent midterm elections, in which Democrats regained control of the Senate and the House of Representatives. This shift could lead to a more aggressive regulatory stance toward the AI industry.

An extended sell-off would let a bit of air out of the stock. If that happens, long-term investors who believe in the company's ongoing momentum can recapture some of the stock's upside at more attractive entry prices.

#3060. 2027 Tax Brackets and Federal Income Tax Rates

Each year, the IRS adjusts tax brackets to keep pace with inflation. The 2027 tax brackets were announced on Nov. 5, 2026.

The IRS has released updated tax brackets for 2027, which apply to the taxes we file in 2028. You may notice that the seven tax rates generally stay the same year-to-year — so why do tax brackets get adjusted?

Well, due to inflation, our money might not stretch as far as it did the previous year. To combat that, the IRS adjusts tax brackets so taxpayers can keep a little bit more of their money in a lower tax bracket compared with the previous year.

For example, a single filer could make up to $50,400 in 2026 and pay a top tax rate of 12%. But in 2027, they could make up to $52,025 and still only pay a top tax rate of 12% thanks to these inflation adjustments.

Not sure how to read the tax bracket tables below?

Find your total taxable income in the "tax bracket" column. The corresponding tax rate is the highest rate you'll pay — it doesn’t apply to all your income; it applies only to the portion that falls within that bracket. The income in the lower brackets is taxed at those lower rates.

For example, let's say you’re a single filer and you expect to earn $150,000 this year. The top rate you’d pay on a portion of your income is 24%. The rest would be taxed at 10%, 12% and 22%.

#3059. Bloom Energy Stock Fell 16% on the Same Day 2 Giant Data Center Deals Named It. Is It a Buy?

Only one of the two deals is new business. Is the stock's drop an opening?

Bloom Energy (BE +3.42%) turned up in two big data center announcements on Nov. 5. NTT DATA said it had agreed to deploy 900 megawatts of the fuel cell maker's onsite power across data center projects around the world.

And Alphabet's Google said Bloom would build the fuel cells for a 702-megawatt plant powering its first data center in Wyoming, a $17 billion project.

The stock had a bad day anyway. Shares ended at $167.52, off around 16%, amid worries that rising opposition to new data centers after the Nov. 3 midterms might slow the build-out of artificial intelligence (AI) data centers.

The growth stock is still up over 90% this year. But it now sits about 52% below the $345.85 record close it set on June 22.

How much new business did Bloom win on Nov. 5? Less than the headlines may suggest, because just one of the two deals is new. I think the other still matters, though, and the stock looks much more reasonably priced than it did in October.

Google won't buy the fuel cells itself. American Electric Power plans to invest around $4 billion in the 702-megawatt project near Cheyenne, Wyoming, the utility said on Oct. 14. It'll sell the plant's output to Google under a 20-year power purchase agreement, and it expects the plant to be fully running by late 2028.

The fuel cell order came months earlier. In January, an AEP subsidiary agreed to buy solid oxide fuel cells for around $2.65 billion for a plant near Cheyenne. The purchase used most of an option for 900 extra megawatts under AEP's 2024 supply deal with Bloom.

For a sense of scale, that one order is worth over half of Bloom's full-year revenue forecast of $4.4 billion to $4.6 billion for 2026.

So, Google's news didn't add to Bloom's orders. The fuel cells were bought in January, and AEP had already named Google as the plant's customer in October.

That still means something, I think. A fuel cell order backed by a 20-year power contract with Google looks more likely to become revenue on time than one linked to an unnamed buyer.

NTT DATA's deal is the new business, and the companies called it Bloom's biggest international project so far. NTT's data center arm plans to use the fuel cells as its main power source, with the relationship meant to reach gigawatt scale across its global portfolio.

What the release didn't say matters, too. It gave no dollar value, no project locations, and no timeline beyond first shipments starting in 2027. This deal shouldn't show up in Bloom's 2026 revenue, and Bloom hasn't said how much of it falls in 2027 or later.

But 900 megawatts is a lot of fuel cells -- nearly a full year of output at the 1 gigawatt of annual production capacity Bloom's Fremont, California, plant had going into 2026. The company planned to double that capacity by year-end.

Bloom needs deals like this, because its quarter-to-quarter growth slowed sharply in the third quarter. Second-quarter revenue of $1.07 billion was 42% above the first quarter's $751 million. Third-quarter revenue then climbed 16% sequentially to $1.24 billion, up 138% year over year. And October's raised outlook (management's third raise this year) implies a sequential gain of 9% to 25% in the fourth quarter.

That's still strong growth. Keeping it up into 2027 and 2028, though, depends on deals like NTT DATA's moving from announcement to shipment.

Based on the midpoint of management's adjusted earnings forecast for 2026 ($3.10 to $3.40 a share), Bloom stock cost around 52 times earnings at its Nov. 5 close. The same forecast had it at around 67 times earnings at the $217.77 close on Oct. 22. On next year's expected earnings, shares now trade at around 31 times earnings.

Still, 52 times earnings is a premium price. It assumes Bloom keeps growing quickly well past this year.

But Bloom landed a big new deal on Nov. 5 as its shares got much cheaper.

Of course, a slower U.S. data center build-out might delay some deliveries. And October's weak spot hasn't gone away. Bloom's operations burned $27 million in cash in the third quarter, mostly because it booked revenue faster than it could bill customers for it.

Is Bloom stock worth buying after a 16% one-day drop? I'd consider buying some shares at this price, a few at a time. And I'd maybe add more when billing catches up with revenue.
