#3058. IRS Announces 2027 Income Tax Brackets, Updated Standard Deduction

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Here's a rundown of changes to some well-known tax provisions for the 2027 tax year.

Each year, the IRS updates common tax provisions, such as income tax brackets and the standard deduction, to ensure the tax code is keeping pace with the rising cost of living.

These tweaks — formally known as inflation adjustments — help prevent “bracket creep,” where taxpayers who received a cost-of-living raise can pay more in taxes even though their purchasing power remains unchanged.

Inflation adjustments for the 2027 tax year were set at 3.2% — larger than the previous two years as inflation heated up. This could mean lower tax bills for some people when they file in 2028.

In the U.S., there are seven marginal rates — 10%, 12%, 22%, 24%, 32%, 35% and 37% — which will remain unchanged for 2027.

Contrary to a popular myth, it’s uncommon for people to pay a single flat federal tax rate on their taxable income. Instead, portions of a person’s income can be taxed at different rates depending on which tax brackets they fall into. For example, some of a person’s income could be taxed at 10%, another chunk at 12%, and so on.

For 2027, the IRS is adjusting those tax brackets — the windows of income that dictate where a tax rate ends and begins — for each filing status. The adjustments mean that some people may be able to keep some of their income in lower tax brackets, and those who received a cost-of-living raise may avoid getting a portion of their income pushed into a higher bracket.

Married filers, for example, can make up to $104,050 to remain with a top tax rate of 12%, compared with $100,800 in 2026. This increase could allow some couples to shelter an additional $3,250 from a higher tax rate in 2027.

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Single filers

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Heads of household

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Married filing separately

» MORE: See the full 2027 tax brackets for each filing status

The IRS lets most filers lower their taxable income by taking the standard deduction or itemizing their returns. The standard deduction, a flat amount based on filing status and age, is popular, as it requires less work to claim and is often more beneficial for those who might not have deductible expenses.

For 2027, the standard deduction will be $16,600 for single filers and those married filing separately, a $500 increase from 2026. Joint filers will be eligible to take $33,200 and heads of household can take $24,925, a $1,000 and $775 increase over 2026, respectively.

» Need help deciding whether to itemize or take the standard deduction? Learn about the differences

Also included in the IRS' 32-page inflation release are various updates to several tax provisions beyond the federal tax brackets. Plus, a brand-new tax credit will go into effect in 2027.

Starting in 2027, taxpayers who contribute to a health flexible spending account (FSA) can contribute up to $3,500 and, if their plan permits, carry over up to $700 into the next tax year.

For those with health savings accounts, the 2027 limit for contributions will rise to $4,500 for self-only coverage and $9,000 for family coverage.

The annual exclusion for gifts, which limits how much taxpayers can give an individual without filing a gift tax return on certain gifts, will be $20,000 per person in 2027, an increase of $1,000 compared with 2026.

The lifetime estate tax exclusion establishes a threshold for the taxation of estates upon a person's death. In 2027, estates valued at or below $15.48 million will not be subject to estate tax, up from $15 million in 2026.

The Education Freedom Tax Credit will launch on Jan. 1. According to the IRS, it aims to support private contributions for K-12 scholarships. Taxpayers who donate to a "scholarship granting organization" in one of the 30 participating states can receive a nonrefundable tax credit of up to $1,700 ($3,400 for couples filing jointly).

#3055. Landstar: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Regulatory shifts and stricter vetting are reshaping Landstar's competitive moat as freight recovery gains momentum.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Landstar (LSTR -1.58%)reported total revenue of $1.512 billion, up 25% year-over-year. Freight capacity is tightening rapidly across truckload sectors as market conditions begin shifting in favor of transportation providers. A resilient balance sheet and a network of independent owner-operators position the company to capture upside during this freight recovery. Three things management said stand out.

Oversized equipment transport is expanding rapidly through demand in data centers, energy, and infrastructure. This specialized capability yields higher revenue per load than standard flatbed cargo.

Recent court rulings on broker liability are forcing independent freight brokers to reevaluate their exposure to litigation. Joining an established network gives independent brokers administrative backing and insurance protection.

Carrier vetting standards have significantly reduced third-party truck count over four years. Eliminating higher-risk carriers helps protect overall margins from severe liability claims and supply chain theft.

Strong capital discipline and fleet reinvestments reinforce the business model as shipping rates normalize.

#3053. The United Explorer Card Is Great if You Fly Basic Economy

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Basic economy tickets come with plenty of restrictions, but holding the right credit card is my top trick for avoiding some of them.

As airfares remain high, that basic economy fare might look more tempting than ever. Saving $100 on a round-trip domestic flight could mean more money for hotels, dining or other experiences at your destination.

I’ve flown my fair share of basic economy flights, which come with restrictions like being unable to select a seat, earning no award miles and boarding last. You also can’t make changes or cancel your itinerary without paying a hefty fee, and — specific to United Airlines — you can’t bring a full-size carry-on on board.

But as a United frequent flyer, I have a secret to share with you: Holding the United℠ Explorer Card (annual fee: $0 intro for the first year, then $150) lets you avoid several of those basic economy restrictions.

Right now, new United℠ Explorer Card applicants can receive this welcome offer: Earn 70,000 bonus miles after you spend $3,000 on purchases in the first 3 months your account is open. Plus, earn 10,000 bonus miles when you add an authorized user in the first 3 months your account is open.

Most major airlines no longer let you earn points or miles on basic economy fares. United is an exception, though — assuming you hold the right credit card.

Eligible cardholders, including those who have the United℠ Explorer Card, earn at least 3 miles per $1 spent on United basic economy tickets, excluding taxes and select fees. That’s half of what a standard economy ticket earns for cardholders (6 miles per $1), but it’s better than zero. If you have elite status, you’ll earn more.

Those miles are in addition to the 3 miles per $1 you earn on all United purchases when using your United℠ Explorer Card.

With the card, your miles can become more valuable. That’s because cardholders get at least 10% off award tickets that are operated by United or United Express. If you have United status, that discount gets bumped up to at least 15% off.

With the credit card, you have access to priority boarding in Group 2. That means you’ll be able to board earlier compared to the rest of the basic economy passengers in Group 6, making it more likely you’ll find space for your carry-on bag.

Now, you might be thinking: Wait a minute… Basic economy doesn’t allow you to bring a carry-on bag. Let me explain how that works.

United enforces its full-size carry-on restriction based on your boarding group. Group 6 passengers can't bring a full-size carry-on.

Because the credit card gets you Group 2 boarding, you essentially skip that restriction.

Premier members — who board in Group 2 or earlier — are also exempt from the full-size carry-on restriction when flying basic economy.

If you don’t want to deal with the hassle of a carry-on bag, you can opt to check it instead.

As long as you pay for your ticket using your United℠ Explorer Card, you’ll be able to check your first bag — and your companion’s first bag — for free when traveling on United. (If you’re booking an award ticket, make sure to use your card to pay for the taxes and fees.)

Keep in mind that if the first flight on your itinerary is operated by another airline, that airline’s baggage rules would apply and you would have to pay for your bag.

Of course, holding the right United credit card only gets you so far.

With basic economy, you’ll still need to pay a fee to change or cancel your ticket, and you won’t be able to select a seat for free.

Ultimately, whether basic economy is a good deal depends on a few factors.

If your plans might change, pay up for standard economy. Otherwise, the penalty to change or cancel will end up costing you more than you saved.

Another thing to consider is whether you’re willing to sit in a middle seat. You might be fine with it for a few hours, but what about a long-haul flight to Asia? Everyone’s threshold is different, so you’ll need to decide whether the middle-seat gamble is worth it.

If your plans are firm and you don’t mind a potential middle seat, booking a basic economy ticket can be a solid way to save money. Just book with a United card like, the United℠ Explorer Card, to avoid the biggest downsides.

You want a travel credit card that prioritizes what’s important to you. Here are some of the best travel credit cards of 2026:

#3050. We Write About Saving Money; Here’s How We Trick Our Brains Into Doing It

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Even personal finance writers and editors need psychological hacks to save money. Here are the tips we use, and why they work.

While staring at the Container Store cart on my phone at 11 p.m., I did something for the first time that I’d read about a hundred times. Instead of spending $200 on more organizational boxes that I categorically did not need, I transferred that amount to my savings account.

Normally, my willpower is no match for the very real psychological phenomenon called present bias: Our brains light up more when we’re rewarding our current selves than our future selves.

Research has also shown that you imagine your future self almost like a stranger — it’s tougher to care about that stranger than about who you are right now. But that night, I saw near-future-me using that $200 on next summer’s vacation. I wanted the vacation more than the boxes. So I abandoned my cart.

I had managed to use a psychological savings trick: Find a goal that helps you envision your future self. With this epiphany in mind, I asked some of my fellow Nerds to share the savings habits they’ve put to work in their own lives. Perhaps reading these can give late-night you some saving inspiration.

Kimberly Palmer, senior writer and spokesperson: I let nonessential items sit in my online shopping cart for at least 24 hours before buying them. It definitely makes me realize I don't need a lot of them! I also informed my children of this rule, and it has helped cut down on their requests — or at least they know they have to be patient.

Karen Gaudette Brewer, head of content: Fellow let-it-sit-in-the-cart person here. I wait for notifications that something's at least 30% off before I make any moves. Ideally, 40% to 50% off.

Julie Myhre-Nunes, managing editor: When I browse online, I add an item to my shopping cart and wait 24 to 48 hours. After that, I either decide it’s a waste of money/an impulse purchase and delete it from my cart (or forget about the cart altogether), or I decide I want it and make the purchase — it’s the former 99% of the time.

Why it works: “Waiting 1 to 2 days allows me to take the emotion out of purchasing. I no longer succumb to marketing or sales, and instead make spending decisions based on what my family and I actually need or can use.” — Julie again, eloquently making the point for me.

Caitlin Constantine, managing editor: My one real shopping weakness is books, and I’ve found I can get a very close approximation of that feeling without spending an outrageous amount of money by going to the library, wandering around the stacks to see what books might look interesting, and checking out a bunch of them. Even if I don’t end up reading them, I’m still helping the library with circulation numbers, so I see it as a win-win.

Spencer Tierney, senior writer: Plus one to Caitlin Constantine’s “my one real shopping weakness is books.” If I want an older or smaller book, I'll try to find it at one of four libraries I belong to on Libby, the library e-book reader app. (The Libby waitlist for new books can take months, so I have to account for that.)

Why it works: There’s no secret here: It’s nice to get free stuff. You might be surprised what the library has to offer, like movies, magazine subscriptions, games, classes and theater tickets.

Pamela de la Fuente, managing editor: We always tell people to automate savings, and I do in my high-yield savings account, but Dasha Kennedy, the woman behind “The Broke Black Girl”, told Elizabeth Ayoola on the Smart Money podcast that you should invest, even a little bit every week or so, for future you. So I just set up a small recurring deposit to my robo-advisor.

Why it works: You only have to make this decision once for your future self, not over and over again. And if you do it early enough, you could avoid being part of the 41% of Americans who wish they had started investing earlier, according to a 2026 NerdWallet survey.

Athena Cocoves, managing editor: To cut down on food delivery, I started cooking when I feel like it instead of when I’m supposed to. I exercise free will and make soup at 10 p.m. It sounds more obvious than it feels, and it's made a big difference in my budget. And it'd make an even bigger difference if I wasn't also rewarding myself with fancy spices and vinegars, but a girl's gotta splurge somehow.

Ryan Brady, lead writer: My wife takes advantage of a T-Mobile freebie that gives us a discount for DoorDash deliveries. She uses it to buy groceries from Aldi instead of takeout. Aldi is far from us. Publix is right across the street, but more expensive. So this saves us money on the actual items and also helps us not impulse buy (as we often do when we're in-store shopping).

Why it works: You don’t need to call on willpower if you can avoid the situation that tempts you to spend money.

» Read more: Strategies to save on groceries that actually work

Kenley Young, managing editor: I use credit cards for most purchases to get those sweet, sweet rewards, but there's a lot in this article that I've also taken to heart.

I've started using library resources more, and it's saving me money on things like monthly subscriptions.

I've negotiated down several monthly bills, too: refinanced my car loan, got cheaper homeowners insurance, opted for a little less auto coverage in exchange for a lower premium, and haggled online with everyone from Xfinity and SiriusXM to my local newspaper until they all gave me a discount.

Plus, I've opted for some home improvement projects with the expectation that my utility bills would be lower. A plumber finally fixed our toilet, which has led to lower water bills, and I even installed solar panels.

Lastly, my analog skill (playing live music) has become a steady side hustle. I play eight to 10 gigs a month, where (on top of my baseline rate) I also get tips, free food and beer.

Why it works: Because you’re Kenley and you put in a lot of effort.

But you don’t need to be Kenley to find a savings habit that works for your personality, your financial situation and even your musical talents. You just need to experiment. Pick the strategy that speaks to you most, and test it out. Which one are you going to try first?

#3049. Portillo's: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Limited-time desserts, not discounts, are the test of whether Portillo's can grow traffic without giving up the margins it now says it is protecting.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Portillo's (PTLO -5.41%) announced a growth strategy focused on operational discipline, closing underperforming locations, and scaling smaller-store designs. For long-term investors, this pivot marks a critical shift away from inefficient expansion toward protecting capital returns and boosting store productivity. Restoring financial rigor across site selection and menu innovation will determine whether the restaurant chain can convert customer brand loyalty into durable profit growth. Three things management said stand out.

Management is overhauling its expansion strategy by shrinking its real estate footprint to reduce capital investment requirements while preserving the potential for high sales volume. The new store layout substantially reduces building size and acreage, allowing the business to lower setup expenses and improve throughput across drive-thru and off-premise channels.

To eliminate cash drains and raise fleetwide margins, leadership is culling low-yield restaurants outside its core Chicagoland market. Pruning these underperforming sites removes historical headwinds from aggressive expansion, freeing up resources to focus on infill growth and adjacent regional markets.

Discounting deep promotions undercuts profits without building lasting guest habits, so management is shifting strategy toward brand-focused limited-time offerings. Premium culinary additions generate customer excitement and drive traffic growth while preserving pricing discipline and restaurant margins.

Fourth-quarter sales momentum is building as limited-time desserts attract guests without broad promotional price cuts. Management is prioritizing technology updates and labor discipline to protect margins in existing stores.

Future enterprise value hinges on executing tighter underwriting standards and maintaining high sales per square foot across a smaller footprint. By realigning capital allocation toward debt reduction and disciplined site selection, leadership aims to earn its right to grow.

#3047. Moderna: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Its proprietary software is built into the cancer drug itself, creating a front-end design moat that rivals may struggle to replicate.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 6, 2026, Moderna (MRNA +5.27%) announced an upgraded full-year revenue framework alongside positive Phase 3 clinical trial data for its individualized cancer therapy. The biotechnology company is demonstrating that its messenger RNA (mRNA) drug-delivery platform can expand beyond respiratory vaccines to high-value oncology treatments while controlling operating costs. Three key takeaways from management explain how this business transition affects long-term shareholders.

Moderna and partner Merck generated positive Phase 3 results for Intismeran autogene, an individualized neoantigen therapy (INT) designed to train a patient's immune system against specific tumor mutations. The clinical trial showed that adding the vaccine to Keytruda, an established immunotherapy, lowered relapse risks in skin cancer patients compared to Keytruda alone.

Management raised its 2026 full-year revenue growth target to between 15% and 25%, up from prior expectations of up to 10% growth. Expanded international supply agreements and stable domestic uptake for mNEXSPIKE, Moderna's updated COVID-19 vaccine, drove the outlook increase while cash spending dropped 10% year over year.

Moderna considers the bioinformatic algorithm that identifies patient neoantigens -- the unique protein mutations on cancer cells -- an integral component of its therapeutic product rather than standard software. This proprietary digital system creates a competitive moat by linking patient sequencing data directly to individualized manufacturing.

Vaccine commercialization is accelerating across global markets, and executive leaders are emphasizing expense control alongside expanding commercial opportunities.

Moderna's long-term enterprise value relies on establishing high barriers to entry through individualized manufacturing algorithms and multiyear supply agreements. Management remains focused on advancing late-stage oncology trials, obtaining international regulatory approvals, and executing Biologics License Applications -- the formal requests for commercial drug clearance -- to sustain commercial momentum.

#3044. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, November 6: A Little Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates dropped today, but inflation data could shake things up next week.

With the midterm elections behind us, mortgage rates are continuing their gradual slide this month. One factor is a sell-off in tech stocks, which can send investors toward the relative safety of bonds and help push mortgage rates lower.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 6.96% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is five basis points lower than yesterday and 11 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Meanwhile, a stronger-than-expected jobs report could slow that slide by reinforcing expectations for another Federal Reserve rate hike in December.

And a reminder: While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

As for what’s next: Tomorrow’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially nudge mortgage rates in either direction. To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the video.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

The recent slide in mortgage rates might reverse course if next week’s inflation data comes in hot.

This week, the Nerds are watching the October Consumer Price Index, which comes out on Tuesday, Nov. 10. CPI is one of two key measures of inflation, which the Federal Reserve aims to keep in check relative to the strength of the labor market. (The Fed looks more closely at the other data set, the Personal Consumption Expenditures Index, which comes out Nov. 25.)

Right now, the labor market is looking pretty darn healthy: The October jobs report, released today from the Bureau of Labor Statistics, showed U.S. employers added 165,000 jobs in October, far more than economists expected, and unemployment edged down to 4.1%.

Raising the federal funds rate is the Fed’s main tool for bringing inflation down, but rate hikes are only on the table when the labor market is strong enough to handle it. Right now, it’s looking fairly likely that the Fed will approve a rate hike when the committee meets Dec. 8-9. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates directly, but an anticipated rate hike can put upward pressure on mortgage rates, which respond to investors’ expectations for the economy and Fed policy.

Still, a jump in mortgage rates isn’t a sure thing. October’s inflation data will capture the effect of the Fed’s last rate hike, which came in the middle of September. If that worked as expected and inflation shows signs of slowing, mortgage rates will be less affected.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.46% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

#3041. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#3039. SpaceX's Starlink Could Build a Nationwide Cell Network. Here's What Investors Need to Know

SpaceX is looking to build a terrestrial cellular network.

Space Exploration Technologies' (SPCX +8.62%) future hinges on its ability to scale its AI division, which comprises more than 90% of the company's total anticipated growth runway. But for now, the space company's Starlink satellite network is generating the most cash flow.

Starlink already provides millions around the world with high-speed internet connectivity. The division has also begun selling bandwidth to terrestrial cellphone networks when users are outside a service zone.

Long term, however, Starlink appears poised to launch its own cellular network. Here's what investors should know.

According to Axios, "SpaceX has spent billions on spectrum and struck a multiyear agreement with Qualcomm to build a 5G network." The outlet also reports that SpaceX may be interested in acquiring an existing terrestrial cellular network to jump-start its user base and acquire additional frequency rights.

SpaceX estimates that the entire Starlink opportunity long-term is valued at roughly $1.6 trillion. Based on the valuations of major U.S. carriers, a successful cellular division could capture several hundred billion dollars of that estimated total value.

But building a terrestrial network won't just be about consumer cellphone plans. As Axios points out, a "combined satellite and terrestrial network could eventually serve autonomous vehicles, delivery drones, robots, and other connected devices. That could give SpaceX opportunities beyond the already competitive consumer wireless market."

In short, building a complementary terrestrial cellular network would help SpaceX not only launch a consumer cellphone offering, but also target the rest of its estimated $1.6 trillion connectivity opportunity. It will likely take years for this process to fully play out, but it's key for SpaceX to realize all of its growth opportunities and justify its lofty valuation.

Ryan Vanzo has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Qualcomm and Space Exploration Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3029. Oklo: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Oklo's plan to sell fuel-fabrication capacity to external reactor operators could turn its fuel business into a standalone revenue stream.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 6, 2026, Oklo (OKLO +22.66%) outlined its strategy to build multi-reactor power fleets while commercializing its internal fuel and isotope capabilities. For long-term investors, the company's integrated model provides control over scarce fuel inputs and deployment schedules. Three things management said stand out.

Oklo's building fuel fabrication and recycling operations to power its own reactors. Management plans to sell that capacity to outside customers, turning fuel into a stand-alone commercial business.

The company completed civil construction and licensing for its Groves-1 test facility in under a year. That project tested internal procedures, supply chains, and safety protocols for larger commercial deployments.

Management is reserving heavy manufacturing slots years in advance to protect future reactor timelines. Securing major forged components early prevents supply bottlenecks during full-scale fleet expansion.

Construction activity is advancing rapidly across power, fuel, and isotope sites. The company leverages its capital reserves to fund long-lead component orders and site infrastructure. Management is closely tracking regulatory conversion timelines between the Department of Energy (DOE, the federal energy department) and the Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC, the commercial nuclear regulator), as well as regional grid interconnection approvals.

By building internal fuel fabrication, power plants, and recycling facilities, the company controls its critical inputs while expanding potential commercial markets.

#3026. Omada: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Omada's cost to serve fell to an all-time low, and it has seemingly found a way to profit from GLP-1 drugs whether employers pay for them or not.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Omada (OMDA -20.37%) raised its full-year guidance for revenue and adjusted EBITDA, or earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization. Member retention reached a record average tenure of nearly two and a half years, which drives recurring revenue and higher customer lifetime value. Lower member care costs prove that clinical scale and software automation generate real operating leverage. Three things management said stand out for long-term investors.

Omada builds digital health tools that help members manage chronic conditions like diabetes and high blood pressure between doctor visits. Patients stick with these multicondition programs far longer than basic weight-loss tracks, creating steady recurring revenue and higher lifetime value, or total expected customer profit.

The business uses artificial intelligence to streamline routine member outreach, allowing human care teams to support more patients without sacrificing quality. As members build lasting health habits, non-GAAP cost of revenue, which excludes stock compensation and measures care delivery expenses per member, fell to an all-time low of $81.

Rising healthcare costs are prompting some employers to reconsider paying for expensive GLP-1 weight-loss medications, which suppress appetite. Omada designed its prescribing options and FlexCare program to deliver clinical support whether employers cover the underlying drug or contract through pharmacy benefit managers (PBMs), which process drug claims for health plans.

Third-quarter revenue reached $91 million with positive net income, and financial results exceeded internal projections. Widespread distribution through major pharmacy benefit managers and direct benefit integrations with health plans create durable distribution advantages as leadership manages spending discipline.

Holding $243 million in cash with no debt gives the business financial strength to expand clinical technology and enter new benefit years.

#3022. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#3015. AMD: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Record cash from the enterprise server push exists alongside a PC segment management expects to shrink, and the two pull in different directions.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, AMD (AMD +11.04%) reported record quarterly revenue of $13.4 billion, driven by expanding adoption of its data center processors. The rapid growth of data center sales to 64% of total revenue highlights a structural portfolio shift toward higher-margin compute platforms. Expanding enterprise demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure positions the company to outpace broader market growth. Three things management said stand out.

Data center server processors -- the EPYC brand of central processing units that run enterprise cloud servers and artificial intelligence applications -- now generate nearly two-thirds of total company sales. As corporate customers adopt EPYC server processors for automated software agents, management expects server revenue to double year over year in 2027.

As high-margin data center shipments scale up, non-GAAP diluted earnings per share -- which measures net income per share of common stock excluding noncash charges -- grew 74% year over year. Higher sales volumes allow the company to manage operating expenses, which are the daily costs of running the business, while expanding operating margins that measure operational efficiency on sales.

Higher memory and component costs slowed sales of personal computer processors to client segments late in the quarter. Management is aligning channel inventory with customer purchases while expanding sales of Ryzen commercial processors to enterprise computer users.

Data center demand is expanding across cloud providers, shifting sales toward higher-margin compute platforms. Deep supply-chain capacity partnerships and memory-bandwidth leadership provide clear structural advantages. Management is pacing inventory while prioritizing research and development to preserve its performance edge.

Long-term growth depends on scaling production alongside supply partners while expanding footprint in enterprise data centers.

#3011. MercadoLibre: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, MercadoLibre (MELI -1.32%) announced an FX-neutral revenue increase of 41% year over year alongside $676 million in operating income. Management sacrificed 40 basis points of operating margin to fund logistics capacity and affiliate marketing. Long-term investors need to know if these upfront spending choices build lasting competitive advantages. Three key takeaways from management explain how these capital choices position the business.

MercadoLibre expanded its fulfillment footprint by nearly 20% in the third quarter ahead of the peak shopping season, incurring upfront costs before shipping volume arrived. Despite these short-term shipping headwinds, shipping cost per unit in Brazil dropped 5% year over year on an FX-neutral basis.

Executive focus remains centered on artificial intelligence integration and proprietary ecosystem features. Internal development efforts prioritize in-house software capabilities over external platform integrations to safeguard customer relationships.

Scale advantages across shipping and payments reinforce the company's competitive moat against regional rivals. Continued capital deployment into fulfillment efficiency and financial services underpins management's multiyear expansion strategy.

#3008. Lululemon Athletica vs. On: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Lululemon's steady profits stand in contrast to On's blistering revenue growth, but its declining sales raise a key question.

Investors face a choice between established market leadership and high-growth momentum when comparing Lululemon Athletica (LULU -0.88%) and On (ONON -0.48%). Both companies are vying for dominance in the competitive athletic category.

Lululemon is known for its high-end yoga and technical apparel, whereas On has built its reputation on innovative running footwear. This comparison explores their financial health and market positioning to determine the better long-term pick.

Lululemon Athletica designs and sells technical athletic apparel, footwear, and accessories through an omni-channel model. It reaches customers via more than 800 company-operated stores and a robust e-commerce platform across multiple countries.

The company also leverages wholesale channels by partnering with fitness studios and university retailers. This diversified approach helps it maintain high brand visibility and reach a loyal customer base in the apparel sector.

In FY 2025, revenue reached nearly $11.1 billion, representing revenue growth of approximately 4.9% compared to the prior year. Net income for the period was roughly $1.6 billion.

On develops and distributes premium performance sports products, primarily focusing on its innovative footwear technology. As of the end of 2025, the company operated nearly 70 retail locations worldwide and maintains a strong presence through wholesale partners and its direct-to-consumer e-commerce platform.

Its growth strategy emphasizes global expansion, with a significant retail footprint already established in China. The company relies on its high-performance brand image to attract runners and lifestyle consumers willing to pay a premium price.

In FY 2025, revenue reached roughly $3.7 billion, which reflects impressive revenue growth. Net income for the fiscal period was approximately $246 million.

Lululemon Athletica faces several legal and operational hurdles, including multiple class action lawsuits regarding its financial guidance and brand health. The company is also navigating a management transition at the CEO position. External factors like changes in U.S. tariff policy and supply chain concentration in Asia add further uncertainty. Increased competition from rivals that can quickly imitate technical designs may also pressure its historical brand premium.

On must navigate the challenges of scaling a global logistics network while competing against much larger footwear giants like Nike. Maintaining its premium pricing and brand exclusivity becomes more difficult as the company moves into broader wholesale distribution. Inventory management is a critical risk, as any miscalculation in demand could lead to heavy discounting that hurts net margin. The company also remains sensitive to shifts in consumer discretionary spending across its primary markets in Europe and North America.

Lululemon currently offers a more conservative valuation profile, while On carries a premium multiple that reflects its significantly faster revenue growth trajectory.

Each company provides slightly different offerings, but both are generally in the fitness and activewear segment. Both are considered premium brands and can struggle when consumers tighten their budgets. They may like Lululemon pants or shoes from On, but in tighter times, a consumer may just trade down to a different brand with similar items at lower prices. That said, there's still opportunity to find the right stocks in that sector of the market. According to Fortune Business Insights, the global sportswear market is expected to grow from $235 billion in 2026 to $402.5 billion by 2034.

For this battle, I would choose On for one main reason. The reason is that On is more in the opening stretch of its journey, with less than 100 stores. In comparison, Lululemon ended 2025 with 811 stores, and it's transitioning into a more mature company, looking for what its next stage will look like. Also, you can see it in the sales growth. For the second quarter of 2026, On reported that revenue increased 13.5%, while Lululemon reported net revenue decreased by 4%. Ultimately, anyone interested in On should dig into its financials a bit more before considering it as an investment. Still, based solely on a comparison with Lululemon, I'd rather own On at this moment in time.

Jack Delaney has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Nike and On Holding. The Motley Fool recommends Lululemon Athletica Inc. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3003. Will this week's blue wave election usher in a Democratic president in 2028?

In surfer slang, a wave that lasts a long time is a "runner." A key question for investors is whether the blue wave that washed over the country Tuesday was that kind of wave, and could it ferry Democrats to victory in 2028.

Statistically, the answer is it should have little effect.

CNBC analyzed elections since 1938 and looked at the biggest midterm wave elections where the party in the White House lost seats to determine whether they portend anything about the presidential election two years later.

Tuesday's election was the biggest House seat loss for the incumbent party in the White House since 2018, but it only barely makes the historical Top 10. It ranks 10th in CNBC's analysis using an estimate of 41 House seats gained by the opposition party as close races are still being tallied. The largest loss came in 1938 when Democrats lost 72 House seats amid a renewed recession, opposition to court-packing by President Franklin Delano Roosevelt and the president's battle with conservatives in his own party. Roosevelt went on to win reelection two years later in 1940.

In more modern times, Republicans swept to power in the House in 2010 two years into Barack Obama's first presidential term, with Democrats losing 64 seats, though retaining control of the Senate. Obama went on to reelection two years later. Of the top four wave elections, which all occurred before a president's final term, the incumbent went on to win reelection each time.

But for the next five waves, the incumbent party lost, including John F. Kennedy beating Richard Nixon in 1960 after Republicans lost seats under President Dwight Eisenhower in 1958; and Joe Biden beating Donald Trump in 2020 after Republicans lost seats in 2018.

Jay Campbell, partner at Hart Associates, the Democratic pollster for the CNBC All-America Economic Survey, said the reason midterm waves may not carry forward is the importance of individual characters in presidential elections.

"A lot can happen in two years. And while presidential elections are certainly subject to the overall political environment, they are also very much about each party's nominee," Campbell said. "If candidate quality matters in congressional elections, it matters 1,000 times more in presidential ones."

It's a long way to 2028 and this wave, gnarly as it may have been, could statistically peter out just as easily as it could wash Democrats back into the White House.

#3002. 3 Super Growth Stocks to Buy Hand Over Fist If the Stock Market Crashes

One of these stocks is up 265% over the past year -- and its shares don't seem wildly overvalued, either.

Is a market crash imminent? No one knows for sure, but on the day that I'm writing this, the S&P 500 has pulled back by more than 4% -- a big move. Will that continue? We can't know. A pullback of some sort, potentially triggering a recession for a while, is likely at some point, though, because crashes and corrections simply happen every few years or so -- and several indicators are pointing to a crash.

You shouldn't keep any short-term money (funds you expect to need within the coming five, if not 10, years) in stocks, and if you're following that guideline, you should plan to just ride out any downturns. Better still, downturns present great buying opportunities. Here are some growth stocks to consider buying the next time the market corrects.

Eli Lilly (LLY -0.79%), home to popular GLP-1 weight-loss drugs such as Mounjaro and Zepbound, has been a blockbuster performer, averaging annual stock price gains of nearly 40% over the past five years. (It's up some 57% over the past year, too, as of early November.) It's no surprise, then, that the stock seems somewhat overvalued, with a recent price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of 14.7, above the five-year average of 13.3.

You could invest in it now if you plan to hold for many years, but buying after a market pullback will get you in at a lower price and boost your eventual gains.

The company is performing quite well lately, posting a 39% year-over-year increase in revenue for its third quarter while management upped its projections for the full year. It's true that its pricing has gone down a bit, but that was more than made up for by increased volume. Lilly also faces competition, but it remains a major player in the GLP-1 world and is expanding there, too, such as via its oral drug Foundayo.

Of course, Lilly is not solely a GLP-1 specialist. It's also home to Kisunla for Alzheimer's disease, Ebglyss for eczema, and cancer drug Jaypirca, among other medications.

Micron Technology (MU +5.12%) is a major semiconductor company specializing in memory and storage chips. It's been posting boffo gains lately, as it supplies memory chips used in artificial intelligence (AI) processing, and AI has been growing briskly. Micron's shares have averaged annual gains of 47% over the past decade, much of that due to outsize gains in recent years. Over the past year, for example, shares are up 265%!

Should you invest in Micron? It's certainly tempting, especially for long-term investors. The shares are even down some 26% over the past month (as of early November). That's due in large part to competition from Chinese company CXMT, as well as worries that the AI and data center build-out might take a breather. But such worries may be overblown, with rival chipmaker SK Hynix seeing a memory shortage persisting through 2030.

Meanwhile, Micron shares seem reasonably valued or a bit undervalued at recent levels, with a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 5, below the five-year average of 11. If you hold out for a market pullback (which may happen this year but could happen later), you'll be getting in at an even more attractive point.

Then there's Nvidia (NVDA +2.77%), the semiconductor titan with a recent market value of $4.4 trillion. It actually seems attractively valued right now, with a recent forward P/E ratio of 13, well below the five-year average of 33. So you might buy into it now if you like what you see after further research.

Shares have averaged annual gains of 52% over the past 15 years, and 65% over the past three years. Growth is likely to continue, though possibly at somewhat slower rates. Nvidia is the biggest supplier for AI model training and inference, and it's expanding its scope to include software, networking, and services.

The company will be reporting its third-quarter results on Nov. 17, and Nvidia has guided for revenue to more than double year over year. If it outperforms, shares could get a nice boost, and if it doesn't, well, arguably, its shares are already down in expectation of that.

Take a closer look at any of these stocks that interest you, and as long as you plan to hold for years, you might buy some shares sooner rather than later. Otherwise, add them to your watch list.

Selena Maranjian has positions in Micron Technology, Nvidia, and SK Hynix. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Eli Lilly, Micron Technology, and Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#2996. Trump says U.S. carried out ‘minor strikes’ on Iran as missiles hit Qeshm Island

President Donald Trump told reporters Friday that the U.S. carried out "minor strikes" on Iran, amid reports that two missiles struck Qeshm Island near the Strait of Hormuz.

The missiles struck the island on Friday, Iranian news agency Tasnim reported, according to Reuters. Tasnim said there was no official information on damage, Reuters added.

The island lies off Iran's southern coast, near the Strait of Hormuz, the vital artery for oil and gas that has become a flashpoint in the war.

Speaking to reporters Friday evening after landing in West Palm Beach, Florida, Trump didn't comment specifically on Qeshm Island, saying: "I don't want to talk about it."

But he added: "We do minor strikes, but they knock out. They built a little bit of an anti-aircraft device, and we knocked it out in about 15 minutes, maybe five." He did not explicitly confirm that he was referring to the reported strikes on Qeshm.

The strikes are the first since Tuesday's midterm elections, after analysts and regional officials had debated whether the vote would lead Trump toward further escalation or renewed diplomacy.

The U.S. struck the same island Monday, in what a U.S. official told MS Now was a "defensive measure."

Trump also told reporters Friday that Iran wants "to make a deal very badly. They have 300% inflation. Their navy's gone. Their airport, everything's gone, and they want to make a deal."

The International Monetary Fund forecast that Iran's consumer prices would rise by an average of 78.6% in 2026, in a report published Oct. 13.

When asked if he wanted to make a deal "really soon," Trump said: "Well, we have nobody getting hurt, nobody getting killed.

"You know, this is a war that we may have been there for seven months, but we probably fought for three or four weeks, really fought. And again, you look at World War One; that's a war. World War Two; that's a war."

Iran has said it wants more than "paper commitments" from the U.S. before entering a new phase of diplomatic talks.

"We must see practical measures by the Americans," Ali Bagheri Kani, deputy secretary of Iran's Supreme National Security Council, told state broadcaster IRIB on Friday, according to Press TV.

#2994. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2992. Magnite vs. Pinterest: Which Media Stock Is a Better Buy in 2026?

One operates the ad-tech backbone for streaming; the other has built a massive online platform. Their financial profiles and risk exposures also tell very different stories.

As digital advertising shifts toward streaming and social commerce, choosing between Magnite (MGNI -0.47%) and Pinterest (PINS -4.19%) requires a close look at where marketing dollars are moving today.

Magnite serves as the technical backbone for publishers looking to sell ad space, while Pinterest provides a visual search environment where users actively look for inspiration and products. Comparing these two companies helps investors understand the different ways to play the ongoing evolution of the digital ad market.

Magnite provides a sell-side advertising platform for publishers and media owners in the media stocks landscape. As the largest independent player in this space, it helps content creators monetize their inventory across connected television, mobile, and desktop. The company maintains critical partnerships with providers like LG Ad Solutions and Telly, allowing programmatic buyers, including The Trade Desk, to access premium video inventory.

In FY 2025, Magnite reported revenue of nearly $714 million, representing a growth rate of approximately 6.9% compared to the previous year. The company achieved a net income of roughly $144.6 million during this period. This is an increase from the net income of approximately $22.8 million reported in FY 2024, reflecting an expansion in its net margin to roughly 20.3%.

Looking further into the financials, Magnite has total cash of $392.8 million and total debt of $411.9 million. Its operating cash flow for the 12-month trailing period is nearly $300 million.

Pinterest operates a visual discovery and shopping platform that now serves approximately 661 million monthly active users. Unlike traditional social media, the platform focuses on curation, making it highly attractive to advertisers in the retail and consumer packaged goods sectors. By integrating shopping features directly into its pins, the company has transformed from a simple image board into a performance-driven marketing channel.

In FY 2025, revenue reached approximately $4.2 billion, which was an increase of nearly 15.8% over the previous year. The company reported a net income of roughly $416.9 million for the year. This resulted in a net margin of approximately 9.9%, which followed a net margin of roughly 51% in FY 2024 when the company benefited from significant tax adjustments and valuation changes.

For a quick glimpse at the financials, Pinterest has $1.4 billion in total cash and $1.2 billion in total debt. Its operating cash flow for the 12-month trailing period is $1.3 billion.

Pinterest faces significant competition for user attention and advertising budgets from Amazon, Meta Platforms, Alphabet, and smaller rivals like Snap (SNAP -2.55%) and Reddit (RDDT +1.38%). The company is currently involved in a securities class action lawsuit related to previous advertising disclosures. Furthermore, Pinterest remains vulnerable to changes in mobile operating systems that limit tracking capabilities, which can make it harder for advertisers to measure the effectiveness of their campaigns.

Pinterest appears to be more of a value, according to traditional metrics.

Pinterest has built up an impressive monthly user base. And there is something to be said for building your own network and controlling that user base. But Pinterest is caught between being a social media site and an advertiser, whereas Magnite's value is more straightforward. Also, between the two, Magnite is more of a growth stock, whereas Pinterest is seeking more stability, with its stock price down 30% over the last 12 months. In comparison, the Magnite stock price is up 81.3% over the same period.

Analysts also have a more favorable outlook on Magnite: of the 18 analysts who rate Magnite, 89% rate it a buy, with 11% rating it a hold. In comparison, the view is more split on Pinterest. Of the 42 analysts who rate PINS, 50% rate it a buy and 50% a hold.

As a growth stock, Magnite could be hit particularly hard if there were a slowdown in the economy. And heading into 2027, we're seeing concerns about an artificial intelligence bubble and mixed signals about the economy's strength. Still, between the two, I would rather own Magnite as a long-term position because of its sales growth opportunities.

Jack Delaney has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms, Pinterest, Reddit, and The Trade Desk. The Motley Fool recommends Comcast and Magnite. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
