#2990. Berkshire shares surge as Wall Street swoons amid AI fears

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway shares roared past the S&P 500 index this week as investors moved money away from slumping tech stocks and moved into what appears to be the relative safety of a traditional conglomerate with relatively little exposure to artificial intelligence amid fears massive spending on data centers won't pay off.

One month ago, Berkshire's widely held B shares were underperforming the S&P by 13.6 percentage points for the year.

With help from three more record-high closes this week, Berkshire is now 5.8 percentage points ahead of the benchmark.

Berkshire more than doubled its share repurchases last quarter to $9.6 billion, while operating earnings declined slightly, the company said on Saturday.

Berkshire was up more than 5% this week while the S&P fell almost 2.7%.

The contrast is even sharper with the Nasdaq Composite, which fell 3.4% on the week.

Thursday's especially heavy tech selling pushed the index down more than 10% below its most recent high in what is commonly referred to as a "correction," but it did make up some of its lost ground Friday.

Two weeks ago, Berkshire's share prices completely erased all of the roughly 15% drop they suffered in the three months following Warren Buffett's surprise announcement at the 2025 shareholders meeting that he would step down as CEO at the end of that year.

Now they are up 6% from the record high closes they hit the Friday before Buffett's revelation on May 3, 2025.

Berkshire is not completely removed from the convulsions around AI.

Abel told us in early September he sees a "significant opportunity" ahead for Berkshire Hathaway Energy to provide electricity to data centers.

He also expected Berkshire would benefit from its big bet on Google parent Alphabet, saying he and Warren Buffett knew AI was "going to have a significant impact on America and businesses," and "we saw Google as a significant player."

Now that investment is getting negative reviews this week as the stock slumps along with other companies related to AI.

As of the end of June, Berkshire reported it owned almost 106 million shares of Alphabet, including 28.6 million shares it bought directly from the company in June: $5 billion of its Class A shares (GOOGL) for $351.81 each and another $5 billion of Class C shares (GOOG) for $348.20 each.

It bought another $7 billion of Alphabet shares on the open market during the second quarter, adding to the position it started building in last year's third quarter, an investment initiated by Warren Buffett.

Bloomberg suggests that "with cracks in the AI trade starting to show, Berkshire's sudden push [into AI] may appear ill-timed."

On June 30, the position had a market value of $37.8 billion and it was the third largest equity holding in Berkshire's portfolio.

Since then both the Class A and Class C shares have dropped by around 20% and the stake is now valued at $30.2 billion, falling behind Coca-Cola's $38.3 billion to the portfolio's #4 position.

(In just over a week, we'll find out whether Berkshire made any changes to its Alphabet position during the third quarter ending September 30.)

Bloomberg quotes CFRA Research analyst Cathy Seifert as saying, "I do think that part of this may be a conscious decision to increase their exposure. One has to ask the question though, is it a little late?"

But the article notes that "for dedicated value investors and longtime Berkshire observers, such jitters may not really matter," with Keefe Bruyette & Woods analyst Meyer Shields saying Abel probably sees bets on AI as a long-term wager on the technology's potential, in keeping with the "conservatism that you would expect at Berkshire Hathaway."

At Barron's, however, Andrew Bary sees what he estimates is a $3 billion loss on Alphabet as part of a "disappointing" long-term trend in Berkshire's stock choices

"The company hasn't created much value from stock picking during the current decade" even though the "S&P 500 has returned 150% since the end of 2019 and tech stocks have quadrupled."

While Berkshire's investments in five Japanese "trading houses," Chevron, and Chubb have done well, "there have been plenty of losers or simply mediocre investments that have been closed out."

He cites Verizon, HP, Paramount (now Skydance), General Motors, Constellation Brands, and Pool.

While he estimates Berkshire's Occidental Petroleum investment is up about 20% from what it paid, OXY is "far behind most energy stocks" amid this year's big increase in crude prices.

Bary also lists stocks that have continued to rise in price after Berkshire cut its stakes, with Apple being the worst offender.

That stake has been reduced by 75% since 2023 at what Bary calculates is an average selling price around $200 per share, well below AAPL's current $342.

Despite those sales, Apple is Berkshire's largest equity holding with a market value of $78 billion. The stock is up 1200% since Berkshire started buying it at the beginning of 2016, at a time when many thought the company had already seen its best days.

Bary thinks Abel will probably keep Berkshire's core equity investments but may concentrate more on his strengths going forward: improving Berkshire's operating companies and making new acquisitions.

His conclusion: "Barring a market rout, equity investing may not be central to Berkshire anymore.

"And if any new Berkshire holdings are revealed soon, it may not be a good idea to buy them. The record hasn't been great."

Berkshire Hathaway's disclosure that it had made a relatively small $27 million addition to its $4.1 billion Sirius XM position helped fuel an ongoing rally that lifted the satellite radio company's shares to a 52-week high this week.

The stock ended 4.9% higher that day at $28.67.

On Friday, Monday, and Tuesday, Berkshire bought more than 924 thousand shares at an average price of $29.10 as the stock continued to gain ground, ending Tuesday at $30.80.

Berkshire disclosed the buys late Tuesday evening, apparently helping to fuel an additional 8.4% gain as the stock closed at $33.40 on Wednesday.

It hit a 52-week intraday high of $33.76 on Thursday but finished lower Friday at $32.40.

Overall, Sirius XM is up 18.6% since the earnings release and has gained 62.0% so far this year.

It's the first addition to Berkshire's stake since late-July and early-August last year.

In 2025, it bought a total of 7.3 million shares at an average price of $21.86.

The position is likely one of portfolio manager Ted Weschler's stocks.

Pacific Power, a unit of Berkshire Hathaway's PacifiCorp, is asking California regulators for a 57% rate increase that would raise the typical residential electric bill by $120 a month.

The Wall Street Journal calls it a "stunning request even in an era of rising electricity costs."

The utility says the hike would be partially offset by a 14% decrease taking effect on January 1.

A lot of the $103 million that would be generated by the increase is already being spent on efforts to make power equipment in the sparsely populated and heavily wooded area more resistant to wildfires.

The utility is facing around $637 million in liability claims for fires in 2020 and 2022.

This week, Reuters reports, Oregon's Supreme Court heard arguments on whether to revive a jury verdict that could expose PacifiCorp "to tens of billions of dollars of potential damages over its role in four Oregon wildfires in 2020."

Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger share their opinions about how artificial intelligence could affect the economy, the government, and how much time people spend on leisure activities.

ANDREW ROSS SORKIN: Thomas Kimay (PH) is here. He's a 27-year-old shareholder from Kentfield, California.

And I should preface this question by saying that he was here 17 years ago at 10 years old, asked you a question from the audience asking you if the internet might hurt some of Berkshire's investments.

At the time, you said you wanted to see how things would play out. He's now updated the question. (Buffett laughs)

"What do you think about the implications of artificial intelligence on Berkshire's businesses, beyond autonomous driving and GEICO, which you've talked about already? ...

WARREN BUFFETT: I certainly have no special insights on artificial intelligence, but I will bet a lot of things happen in that field in the next couple of decades, and probably a shorter timeframe.

They should lead, I would certainly think — but again, I don't bring much to this party. But I would certainly think they would result in significantly less employment in certain areas.

And it may not be good for a given business, but let's take it to the extreme.

Let's assume one person could push a button and, essentially, through various machines and robotics, all kinds of things, turn out all of the output we have in this country.

So, everybody's — there's just as much output as we have. It's all being done by, you know, instead of 150-some million people being employed, one person.

You know, is the world better off or not?

Well, certainly we'd work a lot less hours a week — of work per week and so on.

I mean, it would be a good thing, but it would require enormous transformation in how people relate to each other, what they expect of government, you know, all kinds of things. And, of course, as a practical matter, more than one person would keep working.

But pushing the idea that way is one of the — you'd certainly think that's one of the consequences of making great progress in artificial intelligence.

And that's enormously prosocial, eventually. It's enormously disruptive in other ways. And it can have huge problems, in terms of a democracy and how it reacts to that...

CHARLIE MUNGER: Well, you're painting a very funny world where everybody's engaged in trade. And the trade is, I give you golf lessons and you dye my hair. And that would be a world kind of like the royal family of Kuwait or something.

And I don't think it would be good for America to have everything produced by one person and the rest of us just engaged in leisure.

#2989. Palantir vs. Snowflake: The AI Sell-Off Is Exposing a Critical Difference for 2027

Palantir and Snowflake continue to deliver impressive growth, but soaring AI expectations and renewed market volatility are putting their valuations under scrutiny.

The artificial intelligence (AI) boom has fueled remarkable growth across the technology sector, but the recent sell-off has Wall Street reconsidering how much that growth is worth. For investors looking toward 2027, the pullback presents an opportunity to reassess two leading enterprise AI players: Palantir Technologies (PLTR +3.48%) and Snowflake (SNOW +2.35%).

Both of these companies are benefiting from growing AI adoption, but their distinct business models, financial performance, and valuations present nuanced investment opportunities.

Palantir develops software platforms that help government agencies and businesses integrate complex datasets, improve decision-making, and deploy artificial intelligence. Its Gotham, Foundry, and Artificial Intelligence Platform (AIP) products serve customers ranging from the U.S. military to commercial enterprises.

In fiscal 2025, Palantir generated $4.5 billion in revenue, an increase of 56% year over year. Net income reached approximately $1.6 billion, representing a profit margin of 36.5%. More recent results demonstrate accelerating growth. In the third quarter of 2026, revenue surged 96% to $2.3 billion, while U.S. commercial revenue climbed 148%. Notably, U.S. commercial revenue surpassed U.S. government revenue for the first time, highlighting the company's expanding presence in the private sector.

At the end of 2025, the company carried virtually no debt, with a current ratio of approximately 7.1x. Free cash flow reached $2.1 billion during the year, providing substantial flexibility to fund continued expansion. However, stock-based compensation totaled approximately $684 million, representing 32% of operating cash flow, which could introduce potential shareholder dilution despite the company's strong cash generation.

Snowflake operates a cloud-based data platform that enables businesses to store, manage, and analyze information across multiple cloud environments. Its consumption-based pricing model allows customers to pay for the computing resources they use, while partnerships with Amazon (AMZN -0.35%), Microsoft (MSFT -0.99%), and Alphabet (GOOGL +0.78%) (GOOG +0.78%) support its global reach. As businesses increasingly incorporate artificial intelligence into their operations, Snowflake's platform plays an important role in making enterprise data accessible for AI applications.In fiscal 2026, Snowflake generated approximately $4.7 billion in revenue, an increase of 29% year over year. Still, it was unable to turn a profit and reported a net loss of $1.3 billion, representing a negative profit margin of roughly 28%. However, more recent results demonstrate continued growth. In the second quarter of fiscal 2027, revenue increased 35% to $1.55 billion, while product revenue climbed 37%.

At the end of fiscal 2026, its debt-to-equity ratio stood near 1.2x, while its current ratio was approximately 1.3x. The company generated $1.1 billion in free cash flow, but stock-based compensation totaled $1.6 billion, exceeding operating cash flow.

One of the key risks for Palantir comes from its reliance on large government and enterprise contracts, where lengthy sales cycles and customer concentration can create revenue uncertainty. In addition, its involvement in sensitive defense and surveillance operations also introduces regulatory and reputational risks.

Top risks for Snowflake include intense competition and a consumption-based revenue model that makes growth sensitive to customer spending. Not to mention, ever-growing cybersecurity threats introduce operational and regulatory risks.

Snowflake offers a lower P/S ratio, measuring price against sales over the past twelve months, while Palantir is cheaper relative to future earnings estimates via its Forward P/E.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

From a valuation standpoint, neither stock is cheap. Palantir trades at 103 times forward earnings, while Snowflake commands an even higher multiple of 131 times. Snowflake is considerably cheaper on a price-to-sales basis, at 18.9 times sales compared with Palantir's 54.5 times, but its continued losses and path toward sustained GAAP profitability are less certain.The key difference is that Palantir has demonstrated a stronger ability to turn growing AI adoption into profits. Its latest quarterly revenue surged 96%, driven by accelerating commercial demand, while its strong balance sheet and minimal debt provide considerable financial flexibility.Looking ahead to 2027, Palantir is my preferred buy, although its valuation gives me pause. Investors are paying a substantial premium for future growth, leaving little room for disappointment if the company falls short of expectations. Ultimately, both stocks carry lofty valuations, and the recent sell-off doesn't automatically make either a bargain.

RJ Fulton has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Palantir Technologies, and Snowflake. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#2987. America doesn't like socialism. So why are socialists winning?

Democratic socialists just had their biggest election night yet.

There's just one complication: Most American voters don't actually like socialism.

Six candidates backed by the Democratic Socialists of America won House seats Tuesday, giving the movement its largest foothold yet in Congress and greater clout in a Democratic Party already divided over how far left to go.

The victories, coming on the heels of New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani's rise to power, mark another milestone for a movement that has spent years challenging the party establishment. Now, with Democrats sweeping control of Congress, the question is whether those electoral gains will translate into actual political power.

The results of the new CNBC Voter Poll show the potential hazard of handing too much power to the party's left flank. The survey of roughly 80,000 midterm voters found that 53% view socialism unfavorably, compared with 33% who viewed it favorably. Capitalism fared considerably better, with 56% expressing a positive view. Just 18% of voters identified themselves as democratic socialists.

That disconnect underscores both the opportunity and the challenge facing Democrats and their growing left flank. How the party reconciles the divide between reforming capitalism and fundamentally overhauling it could shape its legislative agenda in Congress, its economic message to voters and, ultimately, its direction heading into the 2028 presidential election.

The DSA's presence in Congress will be quadrupled when Tuesday's victors, all of whom won in safely Democratic districts, are sworn in.

But the projected Democratic landslide of more than 250 House seats could dilute their influence. Party leaders could afford to lose all eight DSA votes on most measures, leaving the group with far less bargaining power than it would wield in a narrowly controlled chamber.

Still, the movement is building financial muscle. While the DSA relies on membership dues, wealthy donors are now backing its candidates through outside groups. The 12 largest donors to American Priorities, a super PAC supporting some DSA-backed candidates, gave nearly $5.9 million this cycle, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission filings through Oct. 31.

"We've had success with more donors but also we have an even stronger ground game to spread our message," said Megan Romer, co-chair of the DSA. "Mailers and ads alone just don't do it anymore. You have to knock on more doors and make more phone calls and talk to more people at work and in your union."

The more revealing tests on Tuesday came outside of liberal enclaves.

In Florida, DSA member Angie Nixon came within three percentage points of an upset in a state where Democrats haven't won a Senate race since 2012 and President Donald Trump won by 13 percentage points in 2024. But she lost by a wider margin than Democratic gubernatorial candidate David Jolly, who took a more moderate approach.

Michigan told a similar story. Progressive Abdul El-Sayed – backed by self-described democratic socialist Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., – won his Senate seat by seven percentage points. Yet he lagged nearly four percentage points behind Democratic Gov.-elect Jocelyn Benson's winning margin.

El-Sayed shares many DSA priorities but neither belongs to the group nor received its endorsement. He says he favors reforming capitalism over replacing it.

State ballot initiatives offered an equally mixed ideological verdict: Nearly two-thirds of voters in ruby-red Montana backed limits on corporate political spending, while deep-blue California rejected a billionaire wealth tax by roughly 10 percentage points, even as voters approved higher income taxes on the wealthy.

The results sharpen the dilemma over Democrats' identity heading into 2028, as Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y., the DSA's most prominent standard-bearer, seemingly weighs a White House bid: Can left-wing populism expand the party's coalition without alienating the swing-state moderates it needs to win a presidential election – especially in a less favorable political environment?

"I think a lot of people did vote for Democrats who would not ordinarily and who probably did it ... with their teeth gritted," Romer told CNBC.

But it's clear that even voters who reject socialism aren't satisfied with capitalism as it stands.

Three out of every four participants in the CNBC Voter Poll said the condition of the U.S. economy was poor or not so good, compared with just 2% who described it as excellent. And 70% said the economic system favors the wealthy rather than treating most Americans fairly.

That frustration helped fuel a populist message far beyond the socialist left, with victorious Democrats from Adam Hamilton in Kansas to James Talarico in Texas tapping into anger over rising costs, economic inequality and the political influence of wealthy elites. Eighteen Sanders-endorsed candidates also won, including in traditionally right-leaning states like Ohio and Montana.

"The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom," Talarico, who flipped a Senate seat for Democrats in the Lone Star state, said in his victory speech. "The people at the top work so hard to keep us angry and divided because our unity is a threat to their wealth and their power."

Even politicians who weren't on the ballot took note. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a video posted to X Friday that "Democrats won by being populist economic fighters."

Economists have pointed to measures showing increasing wealth concentration and a shrinking share of the U.S. GDP heading to workers in the form of pay as signs that the divide between the country's rich and poor is widening. Coming out of the pandemic, the "K"-shaped economy has become a common descriptor for the unequal recovery seen by income classes.

Americans displayed their economic dissatisfaction well before they headed to the polls this week. The University of Michigan's closely followed consumer sentiment index hit all-time lows this year.

"We have a rising tide that is not equally lifting all boats," said Joe Brusuelas, chief economist at RSM, a tax firm. "That's not what we used to tell ourselves when we were young."

Rather than signaling a distaste for capitalism as a whole, Brusuelas said this week's results show voters want a system that also includes populist policies like Medicare for all. Or, as he calls it, "capitalism with a human face."

"These things are not mutually exclusive," said Brusuelas, who regularly briefs members of Congress on policy issues. "We can walk and chew gum at the same time."

Tuesday's Democratic sweep left some on Wall Street racing to prepare their clients' portfolios for potential policy shifts. Bank of America analysts detailed what heightened scrutiny of social media platforms or artificial intelligence data centers could mean for the shares of companies in those industries. CIBC built a basket of stocks that should benefit from a Democratically controlled Congress.

Still, the reaction of Wall Street veterans and New York City business moguls to this week's election was relatively subdued compared with Mamdani's victory in the mayoral race last year.

After Mamdani clinched his party's nomination, Pershing Square CEO Bill Ackman said he was "a bit depressed." Ahead of Mamdani's victory, Kathryn Wylde, who leads the Partnership for New York City, a nonprofit group of business executives, told CNBC that "terror is the feeling" as he rose in the polls.

Wall Street magnates wondered how a city known as the epicenter of financial markets and global capitalism could survive under a self-described socialist. Some questioned if billionaires would flee the city or whether corporations would move their headquarters — fears that largely haven't materialized since Mamdani took office.

With roughly 10 months of Mamdani's term in the rearview mirror, Anthony Chan, the former chief economist at JPMorgan, said finance moguls have realized that politics don't necessarily result in material policy changes. Chan said it's a message they've carried with them exiting this week, with many understanding that any incoming lawmakers in Washington aligned with democratic socialist policies would be a small minority.

"Many of the people that I meet that were very adamantly against and opposed to his candidacy now are sending their kids to his free daycare centers," Chan said, referencing Mamdani's partnership with the state to expand no-cost programs for 2-year-olds in New York City.

"I thought you felt that the world was going to end if this mayor came in," said Chan, who's also an alumnus of Barclays and the New York Federal Reserve. "Now, you're sending your kids to his free daycare centers."

But the DSA has even bigger ambitions for 2028. With growing political clout, infrastructure and financial backing, the movement is looking to expand its influence nationwide, while one of its most prominent allies, Ocasio-Cortez, weighs a White House run.

"That infrastructure is there, and that will make a big difference for a potential left presidential candidate," Romer said. "It's really kind of do or die time. We really have to deliver."

#2986. AMD vs. Intel: Which Is the Better AI Chip Stock to Own for the Next 3 Years Following the Semiconductor Crash?

AMD and Intel stocks have both seen major pullbacks in the past month.

Chip stocks have been absolutely pummeled recently, with Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +11.04%) and Intel (INTC +7.71%) two of the biggest losers over the past month. Meanwhile, the strong Democratic showing in the midterm elections put the artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure build-out on notice.

Despite the political shift, I wouldn't expect there to be a major impact on overall AI infrastructure growth in the coming years, with hyperscalers being able to navigate the current environment and shifting some projects overseas. However, between AMD and Intel, I think the clear winner is likely to be AMD. Here's why.

Advanced Micro Devices has been one of the hardest-hit stocks in the semiconductor pullback. However, the company is riding two powerful waves in inference and agentic AI that are just starting to kick in, and its new Helios rack solution could be a game changer.

Helios is AMD's turnkey AI infrastructure solution that incorporates its graphics processing units (GPUs), central processing units (CPUs), and networking components into a single system. The system contains 72 GPUs and 18 CPUs per rack and is designed to provide the "lowest cost per token" to help reduce overall computing costs. The system has already started being rolled out to key customers, including OpenAI, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, Anthropic, and Oracle, and OEM (original equipment manufacturer) and ODM (original design manufacturer) partners will also soon begin to ship Helios.

AMD has two large deals in place with OpenAI and Meta Platforms for 6 gigawatts of compute capacity each. With each Helios system costing between $5 million and $5.5 million and consuming between 225 kilowatts and 245 kilowatts of power, each deal would be worth approximately $120 billion. Meanwhile, Anthropic's 2-gigawatt deal would be worth around $40 billion, with Microsoft and Oracle as smaller customers.

At the same time, the company is seeing huge demand for its server CPUs, given that these chips are needed to help manage AI agents. Its CPUs are helping run Meta's new Muse AI agents, and the company sees server CPUs becoming a $220 billion market by 2030.

As a result, AMD is projecting growth to soar next year, with it recently forecasting revenue to surge 80% in 2027. The pullback has dropped the stock to a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of around 9 times its 2030 analyst estimates, giving it plenty of room to run the next three years.

While AMD has been the victim of a broader semiconductor stock sell-off, much of Intel's wounds are self-inflicted. This is due to the company's still-outsized exposure to the PC market, which is seeing weak demand as high memory costs have caused computer makers to raise prices, slowing demand.

Intel's client computing and physical AI group (CCPG) remains its largest segment, and revenue for the unit fell 5% last quarter. However, the company is expecting units to drop 10% in 2027 after a 12% decline this year, which will also lead to a large inventory correction that will significantly impact the next two quarters.

Meanwhile, Intel's foundry business continues to produce large losses, including a $1.9 billion operating loss in Q3. The company is struggling to find external customers, in part due to technology and in part due to conflicts of interest, and if the data center build-out shifts overseas, this will likely sting it even more, given its strong domestic footprint.

Intel is the market leader in server CPUs, and like AMD, it's been seeing strong growth in this area. However, the company has been consistently losing shares to AMD and custom CPUs. This is only expected to continue in the years ahead, especially as the company has no turnkey solution like Nvidia and AMD.

As such, I think the stock could continue to underperform over the next three years.

Geoffrey Seiler has positions in Advanced Micro Devices and Meta Platforms. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Advanced Micro Devices, Intel, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, Nvidia, and Oracle. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#2983. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2981. Trump targets Slotkin in call for DOJ probe of alleged Democratic threats to Republicans

#2979. Risk of Iran war escalation remains high despite Democratic sweep in U.S. midterm elections

The U.S. and Iran are careening toward renewed war despite growing hopes this week that the Democrats' decisive midterm election victory will bring the conflict to an end, analysts say.

Crude oil prices fell more than 16% over the last five trading days after U.S. Vice President JD Vance publicly blamed the Republicans' stinging loss on the war. Vance's remarks raised questions about whether the administration still has support to continue the conflict with Iran.

The Democrats plan to use their comfortable majorities in both chambers of Congress to pass a war powers resolution that demands President Donald Trump end the war. But tensions in the Middle East have not eased since the election, and the Democrats aren't sworn in until January, the analysts say.

"Throughout the week, we were struck by the divergence between deepening escalation concerns in the Gulf and the headline-driven off-ramp optimism of market participants thousands of miles away," Helima Croft, head of global commodity strategy at RBC Capital Markets, told investors in a note Friday.

Late Saturday, U.S. Central Command said it disabled a St. Kitts-flagged ship after the tanker ignored multiple warnings and attempted to leave the region after visiting an Iranian port, according to media reports. Iran state media also reported on explosions on Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, as well as several blasts occurring in the port of Jask. CNBC couldn't independently confirm the reports.

Several oil tankers have also come under attack near Oman since the election, killing one seafarer, according to maritime security agencies. Iran's Houthi allies in Yemen continue to fire missiles at neighboring Saudi Arabia.

Meanwhile, the U.S. military buildup continues, with a third carrier strike group en route to the region. The consulting group Rapidan Energy sees an 80% chance that fighting will resume as diplomacy stalls.

Trump told reporters Thursday that he will make a decision soon on whether to attack Iran again.

"I would rather not destroy the rest of the country if possible but maybe I'll have to," Trump said in the Oval Office. "You'll know soon enough."

"We're locked and loaded," the president said. "If we have to do the rest of the Iran thing, we're very much locked in."

Crude oil prices will vault above $100 a barrel again in response to renewed fighting, said Bob McNally, president of Rapidan and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush. Brent crude, the international benchmark, traded above $97 on Friday, while U.S. crude was around $90.

"President Donald Trump is determined as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it," the analyst said. The escalation dynamic remains in place so long as that core difference between the U.S. and Iran persists, he said.

The risk of war is still alive, especially since Trump no longer faces future elections, said Amos Hochstein, a former energy advisor to President Joe Biden. Gasoline, diesel and jet fuel prices would all surge as a consequence, Hochstein told CNBC's "Power Lunch" Friday.

Tehran has been taunting the Trump administration since the election. The social media account of Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei said the "process of the U.S.'s collapse has accelerated" after the midterm results.

And Congress is unlikely to pass a war powers resolution until Democrats take control in January. Trump can veto such a resolution anyway.

The main leverage Congress will have is the power of the purse, Croft said. Government funding expires on Dec. 11, and negotiations for another continuing resolution could drag into 2027, she said. This would give Democrats more leverage when they take power in January, the analyst said.

"Importantly, deferring funding decisions would not immediately constrain Trump's ability to conduct military operations, but could significantly reshape the administration's longer-term funding outlook," Croft told investors.

Trump discussed diplomatic efforts to de-escalate tensions in the region with the emir of Qatar during a Friday phone call, according to Qatar's state news agency.

An unnamed senior Iranian official told Reuters on Thursday that Tehran sent a nuclear proposal to the U.S. through Qatar. Tehran will only discuss the proposal after the U.S. meets its demands, the Iranian official told Reuters.

Trump seemed to dismiss the diplomatic efforts Thursday.

"Iran will never have a nuclear weapon," the president said. "They're not doing anything without our approval. It doesn't make any difference what they're looking at," he said of the diplomatic efforts.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

#2970. Meta chief strategy officer Dave Wehner leaves to join AI infrastructure startup Fireworks as CFO

Dave Wehner, Meta's chief strategy officer, is leaving the company for four-year-old AI startup Fireworks.

At Fireworks — an AI inference and model-training platform focused on open models that was valued at $17.5 billion during a Series D fundraising round in July — Wehner will assume the CFO role, he announced on X on Friday.

Wehner will be tasked with scaling the company's finances and operations, Fireworks CEO Lin Qiao said in a statement to CNBC.

"Fireworks has the right mission for the moment," Wehner wrote on X, adding: "I've become increasingly bullish on what they are building. There's a remarkable depth of technical talent here, coupled with a very practical focus on what customers need."

The AI startup, founded in 2022 by former Meta and Google employees, is a platform that lets developers customize and run open models for their businesses. Its latest investment round was led by Atreides Management, Index Ventures and TCV, but Fireworks is also backed by Nvidia and Sequoia Capital. Business Insider reported that it is in early talks to raise funds again at a possible $30 billion to $40 billion valuation.

Wehner joined Meta, then called Facebook, in 2012 and was the company's chief financial officer from 2014 to 2022.

"Dave helped guide Meta through tremendous growth and complexity over 14 years, and brought disciplined thinking to some of our hardest company-level decisions," Meta COO Javier Olivan said in a statement to CNBC.

Fireworks is notably smaller than Meta — the tech giant has about a $1.56 trillion market capitalization as of Friday's close — and its competitors, which include OpenAI and Anthropic.

"Dave told me he wanted to get back to a smaller company where he could build toward a big impact again," Fireworks' Lin wrote in a LinkedIn post on Friday. "He sees AI as the defining technology shift of his lifetime and wants to help shape what comes next."

The company's size has not made it immune to the risks and complications often associated with AI technology. In October, the company found "a security incident involving unauthorized use of internal Fireworks credentials," in which "an unauthorized third party accessed environment variables used in evaluation jobs in a limited number of customer accounts," it said in a press release.

"Based on our investigation at this time, we have not detected evidence of unauthorized access to customer models, datasets, training data, personal information, or inference traffic within the Fireworks security perimeter or control," the release said.

#2967. Brands are sponsoring IRL communities. Some experts say that could spell their end

On a brisk Saturday in October, more than 80 people try to shuffle into the small Corner Bookstore in New York City's Upper East Side. They're part of local literary community Lit Club NYC's quarterly book crawl. Today's event, where some attendees are "slightly over sci-fi" and others are in their "rom-com era," is sponsored by Tinder.

The Lit Club-Tinder partnership was facilitated by Offline, a platform for connecting hosts of gatherings across the U.S. with advertisers keen to sponsor their events. The goal is to help advertisers "deliver outsized media performance and drive growth," according to Offline's website. Offline founder Andrew Roth, 28, is also keen to help hosts ensure their gatherings are sustainable long term.

Kelly Chervin is just such a host. Chervin, 35, founded Lit Club in March 2025 as a way to meet other Latina readers and connect over literature. But she has bills to pay. "If we want to have a literary community be around sustainably," says Chervin, "then paid partnerships are part of that."

For the Tinder collab, Chervin makes $20 per attendee, and must throw in deliverables like social media posts highlighting the partnership. On Lit Club's Instagram, a recent post included the line "You can now discover events like these on the @tinder app!"

Whether community members will stay connected when a sponsor gets attached remains to be seen. Some experts are optimistic about the opportunity this kind of marriage presents, while others are certain it will eventually bring about communities' demise.

Either way, many intimate gatherings around the country are quickly adding a sales pitch.

Roth began his career by founding Gen Z-focused research and consulting company DCDX while studying at Vanderbilt University in 2020. The company has worked with major brands including Chipotle, Hinge and Spotify and was recently acquired by Visible Ventures for an undisclosed sum. Roth will stay on as a Board Advisor.

In 2024, while still at DCDX, Roth had the opportunity to work on an advertising campaign for fragrance company Le Monde Gourmand at in-person events with communities like those on which Offline now focuses.

He says it made him wonder: "Can we bring those [advertising] dollars into the real world and help these communities be preserved?" In September 2025, he and fellow entrepreneur Chris Banks officially launched Offline.

The company's platform enables hosts of gatherings across the country, from mom groups to run clubs to literary circles, to create a profile including details about how often events happen and how many people tend to show up. Brands can then use the site to match with profiles and events that fit their target audience and create a brief for a campaign that hosts can submit ideas to.

Offline now features more than 900 communities altogether, says Roth, with hosts making an average payout of $2,500 to $3,000 per event.

"The language we always use is 'intimacy at scale,'" he says.

For hosts like Chervin, the opportunity to generate revenue is critical. Lit Club's current income streams include ticketed events, a monetized newsletter and brand partnerships.

Chervin is choosy about the partnerships she takes on, she says. They have to feel organic. The Tinder tie-in, for instance, checks a couple of boxes: "Tinder really wants to move to in-person connection," she says as an example, which is at the core of her mission as well.

Michael Lombardo, 43, discovered bird watching in 2019 and was immediately struck by how fun and present the activity was. It made him feel "like the eight-year-old version of myself," he says, "where I was actually inhabiting the world."

Lombardo founded the McGolrick Bird Club in April 2023. Named for Brooklyn, New York's McGolrick Park, the free, weekly meetup convenes every Saturday morning, when he and others go bird watching. The event now has about 100 weekly attendees, and many of them are repeat guests.

Lombardo recently started dabbling with brand partnerships and connected with Offline earlier this year. For him, any partnership would have to benefit the community in some way, he says. But as a freelance graphic designer and writer, it's something he'll consider given the bigger picture of his life and priorities.

"I do need to support my family," he says. "I have two children."

Experts differ on what they think introducing the element of advertising to the groups could mean for the human connection they provide.

Amit Kumar, an assistant professor of marketing, psychological and brain sciences at the University of Delaware, is hopeful about the potential of these events, regardless of a brand's involvement. For him, the point is to create as many opportunities as possible for people to meet.

"As long as they actually are allowing people to engage with one another and connect with one another," he says, "there's a potential benefit to that."

Professor of sociology at Cornell University Benjamin Cornwell is not so sure. A community that got together organically through a genuine, shared interest is no longer organic once fueled and harnessed by a profit-motivated company, he says.

When that happens, community organizers risk introducing unwelcome messaging. Members may "lose faith in the original purpose," he says, adding that some will even "drop out." That won't necessarily happen immediately, he says. But, "it's inevitable."

Back at the book crawl, attendees' opinions differ about Tinder's involvement.

Some think it's cool Tinder is branching out into IRL events. Others don't like the implication that the event is somehow a ruse to meet a significant other, rather than a way to just meet people in general. Some people don't mind as long as the company doesn't feel too pushy in its agenda.

In September, Chervin partnered with an AI company at one of her events and appeared in an ad for Meta's AI tool Muse.

But among her larger community, she says, "a few people did tell me they don't think they can come back."

Roth stands by the financial ecosystem Offline is trying to build.

"If 10 years from now people look at us and say, 'Oh, these are the evil guys that bastardized IRL,' so be it," he says. "But I'll look at that and say, you know, we paid out a billion dollars to organizers, and we've created I don't know how many more events."

#2965. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2964. Berkshire Hathaway more than doubles buybacks in third quarter as earnings decline

Berkshire Hathaway said Saturday it accelerated its share repurchases during the third quarter, as operating and overall profits took a hit.

The Greg Abel-led conglomerate bought back $9.6 billion worth of Berkshire stock. That's more than double the $4.5 billion repurchased in the second quarter and brings the company's total buybacks for 2026 to about $14.4 billion.

The sharp increase came as Berkshire's operating earnings — those from wholly-owned businesses — fell year over year, as profits from its insurance business pulled back.

Berkshire operating profits totaled $12.705 billion, down 5.8% from $13.485 billion in the year-earlier period. Insurance underwriting tumbled 43% year on year to $1.352 billion, while insurance investment income declined 14% to $2.734 billion. Berkshire's railroad, energy and manufacturing businesses all saw earnings grow year over year.

The company's overall earnings, which include Berkshire's sprawling stock market portfolio, also dropped 28% in the third quarter to $22.112 billion from $30.796 billion a year prior. Investment gains for the company fell nearly 50% to $9.407 billion.

Despite the earnings pullback, Berkshire stock has outperformed recently amid a massive rotation out of technology stocks. Class A shares are up more than 12% over the past month, while the S&P 500 has dropped 5.5% in that time. Year to date, Berkshire has gained 13%, while the broad market index is up just 7.8%.

Berkshire's five largest equity holdings remained the same in the third quarter: Alphabet, American Express, Apple, Bank of America and Coca-Cola.

Berkshire's cash hoard also grew 3% in the third quarter $377.435 billion from $365.514 billion in the second quarter. However, excluding rail and utility cash along with T-bill payables, the conglomerate's cash holdings totaled $352.3 billion, down 1.9% quarter over quarter.

— CNBC's Alex Crippen contributed reporting.

#2960. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

#2959. Anthropic IPO meets AI backlash after midterms show stinging rebuke of data centers

Americans were unequivocal at the ballot box this week as they expressed disdain for tech billionaires and mistrust of artificial intelligence. With that backdrop, Anthropic is about to try to go public.

The AI company is gearing up for a potentially historic IPO, likely targeting mid-November for its Nasdaq debut. CNBC reported on Friday that Anthropic investors see the company aiming for a valuation of $1 trillion to $1.5 trillion, down from a target of $2 trillion that had been floated in recent months.

From Wall Street to Main Street, it's becoming a tougher environment for Anthropic. The Nasdaq on Thursday suffered its steepest drop in 19 months, and it's down to start November after October marked its worst month since March 2025. Chipmakers and neoclouds, the companies most closely tied to the AI buildout, are getting hit the hardest.

Voters rebuked AI in Tuesday's midterm elections, while sending Democrats to sweeping congressional victories across the country. Candidates who advocated for more stringent AI regulation won handily in states like Michigan, Ohio, Texas and Georgia, which will all now have Democratic governors, and local communities opposed data center-related ballot measures in 40 out of 41 cases.

More than half of voters said AI data centers were a "crucial" or "major factor" in who they supported for Congress, according to an exit poll from NBC News.

"People don't want a data center going near them if it's just going to house AI that's going to help a big tech company profit," Maura Austin, a behavioral scientist at the University of Virginia, told CNBC. "What Anthropic is doing when they're going public with such a high valuation is they're really leaning into that big tech, billionaire kind of caricature. I expect that's not going to go well with the public."

Anthropic has long tried to position itself as a safety-conscious company that takes AI's potential risks and benefits seriously. CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company alongside a handful of researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

The company's enterprise focus has helped it avoid becoming the public face of AI, leaving that distinction to OpenAI and CEO Sam Altman. But with Anthropic poised to become the first pure-play AI company to hit the public market, minting a number of new billionaires and many more millionaires in the process, a heightened level of scrutiny is coming.

"Being a public company, just that by itself pushes you in the limelight," Reena Aggarwal, a professor of finance at Georgetown University, said in an interview. "And then AI is such a hot-button issue that Anthropic will be in the limelight even more."

Matt Kennedy, a senior IPO market strategist at Renaissance Capital, said that's not necessarily a bad thing for Anthropic.

Public markets force greater transparency, he said, and the company could attract new users through its debut. And since AI has become such a mainstream conversation topic, several private companies are already household names.

"I would say that a public company does not deserve more criticisms than a comparable private company," Kennedy said. "So Anthropic should not be put in a harsher public spotlight than OpenAI just because it's public."

AI backlash accelerated over the summer after a series of high-profile safety and security incidents rattled the public. As concerns mounted, the debate over data centers intensified, while tech's hyperscalers simultaneously boosted their forecasts for capital expenditures, each reaching into the hundreds of billions of dollars.

Anthropic and OpenAI depend on state-of-the-art data centers to train models and serve customers, and they're trying to build out capacity as quickly as possible to meet demand. Anthropic said in an amended IPO prospectus this week that it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Americans grappling with inflation and stubbornly high gas prices have grown worried that the facilities could exacerbate environmental harms, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss. For many, a proposed data center in their backyard became the physical manifestation of the widespread antipathy toward AI.

In the risk factors section of its prospectus, Anthropic said opposition to data center development has become more organized across jurisdictions, and that the company's public profile could draw particular scrutiny to projects associated with it.

Nicole Greene, an analyst at Gartner, said she expects that Anthropic's executives have long been prepared to contend with AI backlash, especially given the disruptive nature of the technology.

"There's going to be big emotions," Greene told CNBC. "With big emotions and feelings — fear and excitement — comes heightened scrutiny."

Amodei acknowledged the souring sentiment around AI in a post on X in August. He said it's the result of a "crisis of trust," and that people believe companies, the government and the tech industry are always "cooking up some new way to screw them over."

Rebuilding public confidence is going to be an uphill battle as the company begins providing quarterly financial updates.

The University of Southern California published a survey on Monday that found that 71% of respondents don't trust big tech companies to act in society's best interest "at all" around data center development.

"Once you've lost trust, it's hard to gain it back," Wändi Bruine de Bruin, a professor at the University of Southern California's Schaeffer Institute, said in an interview. "The numbers are not good."

#2955. Open Thread: Jeremy Sochan’s first press conference after being drafted first overall in the G-League

What a ride Jeremy Sochan has had, and the new season is just getting underway. After nearly four seasons with the San Antonio Spurs, he was waived and signed with the Knicks, picking up a championship ring when New York beat the Spurs in the 2026 Finals.

After New York, it appeared Sochan was spending the 2026-2027 season in Portland, but was waived after four preseason games. The Polish-American power forward declared for the G-League draft and one week later was chosen first overall by the Texas Legends.

Here he is earlier this week in his first press conference as a member of the Legends.

"I think the way I grew up, my family, my parents; it's always been like that. I could look at it negatively (the Portland situation). But when I look back at that situation, I think the two months I was there, I grew a lot. I was healthy throughout the whole training camp, preseason and open gym. So I think that was a blessing. When we got into preseason games, the minutes I got I think I used them to the best of my ability. I had good games. So what would be the point of being negative and angry for getting waived? Of course, you can look at it as that's the NBA and I have a contract. But at the same time I have good momentum. So, why would I hold weight on something that I can't control? And instead, be in this moment and do what I can do and keep that momentum going."

According to Marc Spears, Sochan originally found out he was being waived by the Spurs through another player:

"I thought I was going to be there for the rest of the season at least. And a few days after the trade deadline, it's funny. I don't want to be messy or anything, but I found out from one of my teammates that they wanted to waive me, and it definitely hit me in a way. But again, I kind of had the mentality of, 'It is what it is.' I heard it was pretty possible from someone else, and then officially found out through my agent. So, that was definitely new to me."

Texas Legends season kicks off tonight in Mexico City against the Capitanes.

#2953. Agencies publish resolution plan feedback letters for 15 banking organizations

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. The agencies conducted a joint review of the 2025 resolution plan submissions from 15 banking organizations with over $250 billion in assets.

In addition, the agencies determined that the shortcoming previously identified in the 2021 BNP Paribas resolution plan has been satisfactorily addressed.

#2952. Why the prognosis for healthcare stocks is uncertain ahead of Congress’ new makeup

Healthcare stocks may struggle to gain momentum as Democrats prepare to take over Congress.

Jared Holz, who covers healthcare for Mizuho, says the outlook for the sector is uncertain because of likely gridlock between lawmakers.

"The next couple of years I really can't see too much happening," the firm's sector specialist told CNBC's "Fast Money" on Wednesday. "I'm not sure you want to own everything, but you can pick and choose the names… Pharma is trading more expensive than tech for the first time in 20 years. So, I backed away from pharma. I would look at medtech."

The State Street Health Care Select ETF (XLV) jumped 3% a day after Election Day.

"You had this very weird situation where typically you wouldn't have all of these categories work in tandem, and they did. So, I think some things just a little bit off," said Holz, a day before healthcare got swept up in Thursday's Wall Street sell-off.

According to Holz, healthcare's real booster shot may come if a Democrat wins the White House in 2028.

"Then I think you see some of the Trump strategies rolled back. That's where I think healthcare could actually perform well," said Holz, who added it hinges on Democrats retaining the Senate and House.

For investors who are long on healthcare, Holz said each subsector should be treated differently.

"Can we get some of the regulation that has taken place over the past couple of years and overturn that over the near term? I don't necessarily believe so," Holz said. "But if you do, then you probably want to be long some of these medical device stocks that are at or near multi-year lows."

The State Street Health Care Select ETF ended virtually flat this week. It's up around 32% since President Trump took office in January 2025 — beating the S&P 500 by almost 9 percentage points.

#2949. Against Oklahoma City, the Wizards were the bug

Oklahoma City Thunder broadcaster Michael Cage summed up this game perfectly: "In this game, sometimes you're the bug, and sometimes you're the windshield." In this one, the Wizards were in the role of the bug.

Washington was no match for the Thunder. Oklahoma City took control early and the Wizards had no answer for their athleticism, execution, and swarming defensive presence.

The Wizards converted just 29.6% of their field goal attempts, including 7-43 from three-point range. That's 16.3%. Which is bad. Very bad.

Positives for the Wizards were scarce. Among them — Alex Sarr grabbed 14 rebounds in 28 minutes. Bub Carrington shot 3-3 from deep. Bilal Coulibaly defended Shai Gilgeous-Alexander pretty well in the first quarter — SGA had two points on o-3 shooting when Coulibaly left the game. He immediately started scoring and finished the game with 30 points and 9 assists.

For Washington, it's a third consecutive loss — their first three-game skid of the season. Before the game, head coach Brian Keefe talked with reporters about the team figuring out their formula for success. The team would be wise not to over-index on this game. It was a bad result, and the team looked awful.

Despite missing Jalen Williams and Chet Holmgren (in some order, their second and third best players), OKC is a special case. It's a championship-level team with something to prove. The Wizards need to learn to compete at that level, and the lesson of their last three games is that they're still not close.

That's not the end of world 10 games into the season. But they need to internalize that lesson and do the work to become competitive against high-level opponents.

Kudos to the OKC stadium operations team — when the Wizards starters got introduced, they played Harry Potter theme music. Good job.

The Wizards entered the game with the NBA's second best "in the paint" field goal percentage (62.2%). The Thunder had the NBA's second best defensive paint FG% (holding opponents to just 50.2%). Safe to say OKC won that battle.

In the first quarter, the Wizards ran a pick-and-roll option out of a horns set for AJ Dybantsa. He had a choice of going left or right. It didn't go great — he ended up battling his way into a mid-ranger, which he missed. The set was good, though.

Tre Mann got real minutes for the first time this season. He looked pretty good — quick, aggressive, athletic. I'd like to see more.

Jotted during the second quarter (could have been jotted anytime) — OKC defenders switch HARD.

Another second quarter note — OKC was doubling Trae Young to get the ball out of his hands. The other Wizards weren't able to take make plays, in part because OKC defenders hustled back to their matchups and rotated so perfectly.

I thought maybe the Wizards had a chance of making things respectable — a thought that disappeared when I saw their possession of the second half. It was a pick-and-roll for Kyshawn George, who got caught in no-man's land. The only teammate who cut was Coulibaly, who didn't get free. That should have triggered a cascade of movement, but the other guys stood around until George was forced to take a difficult shot, which he missed.

The Wizards tried a small-ball lineup in the second half. It got shredded by Aday Mara and the Thunder.

#2947. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2946. In pictures: France protests draw 146,000 as clashes erupt for fourth straight weekend

Protesters clashed with police in France on Saturday as demonstrations over living costs and public services entered a fourth consecutive weekend and drew students, trade unions and members of the revived "Yellow Vest" movement onto the streets.The country's Interior Ministry counted 146,000 demonstrators nationwide, including 46,000 in Paris. A youth union alliance put attendance at 250,000 nationally and 75,000 in the capital. At least 360 people were arrested across the country, AFP reported, citing a ministry tally.

Police used tear gas against people throwing projectiles as the main Paris march ended at Place de la Nation, per AFP. Garbage cans were set alight as protesters and police clashed in several cities, according to Le Monde.

Police blocked a separate, banned Yellow Vest gathering at Place de la Concorde and arrested two movement leaders, Le Monde reported.

The Yellow Vests emerged in 2018 in opposition to fuel-tax increases, before broadening into a movement protesting high living costs and the policies of President Emmanuel Macron.

The turnout was lower than on the previous three weekends. It was also below union-led demonstrations on Nov. 5, when the Interior Ministry counted 575,000 people nationwide.

The protests began in late September with high-school students demanding better conditions, before university students and unions joined.

This weekend's protests come after French lawmakers rejected the revenue section of the 2027 budget, as the government wrestles with addressing public anger while trying to rein in its finances.

The government's original proposal now goes to the Senate, which is scheduled to begin debating it Nov. 20.

Prime Minister Sébastien Lecornu is due to meet labor and employer representatives Monday to discuss wages and purchasing power.
